6.6    Use of the Baseline Privacy Extended Header in the MAC Header
If encryption is not enabled on a particular downstream traffic flow (e.g., a CM's unicast traffic or a particular IP
multicast group), then the CMTS SHOULD NOT place a Baseline Privacy EH element in the frame.
If encryption is not enabled for a CM's unicast traffic, then the CM MUST include the Baseline Privacy EH element
with the ENABLE bit set to 0 in fragmented upstream frames.
If a CM sends a MAC frame consisting of only a MAC header and, optionally, an EHDR, then the CM MUST
disable encryption on that frame. If a Baseline Privacy EHDR is present on such a frame, then the CM MUST set the
ENABLE bit to zero (0).
If a CMTS sends a MAC frame consisting of only a MAC header and, optionally, an EHDR, then the CMTS MUST
disable encryption on that frame. If a Baseline Privacy EHDR is present on such a frame, then the CMTS MUST set
the ENABLE bit to zero (0).




09/03/25                                             CableLabs®                                                         35
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                         Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


7 BASELINE PRIVACY KEY MANAGEMENT (BPKM) PROTOCOL
7.1       State Models
7.1.1      Introduction
The BPKM protocol is controlled by two separate but interdependent state machines, described in this section: an
authorization state machine and a TEK state machine. The state machines are presented here for explanatory
purposes only and are not to be construed as constraining an actual implementation. However, the external behavior
of CMTS implementations MUST be identical to the state machines described in this section. The external behavior
of CM implementations MUST be identical to the state machines described in this section.
CM authorization, controlled by the authorization state machine, uses the following process.
      •    The CMTS authenticates a client CM's identity by validating the signature on the Authorization Request
           message (BPI+ V2 only) and by verifying the certificate chain of the device certificate used to sign the
           message with.
      •    The CMTS provides the authenticated CM with ephemeral key exchange parameters (BPI+ V2) or with an
           authorization key (BPI+ V1) in the Authorization Reply ("auth reply") message, from which a KEK and the
           message authentication keys are derived,
      •    The CMTS provides the authenticated CM with the identities (i.e., the SAIDs) and properties of primary
           and static SAs for which the CM is authorized to obtain keying information.
      •    The CM authenticates the received parameters by verifying the message signature and the certificate chain
           from the serving CMTS and sends an auth status info message to the CMTS that contains the result code of
           the CMTS authentication performed by the CM (BPI+ V2 only).
The KEK is a two-key, 3DES encryption key that the CMTS uses to encrypt TEKs that it sends to the CM. TEKs are
used to encrypt and decrypt user data traffic and REG-REQ-MP MAC management messages. The CM and CMTS
use message authentication keys to authenticate, via a keyed message digest, the Key Requests and the responses
that they exchange.
After achieving initial authorization, a CM periodically seeks reauthorization with the CMTS; reauthorization is
managed by the CM's authorization state machine. A CM maintains its authorization status with the CMTS in order
to be able to refresh TEKs. TEK state machines manage the refreshing of traffic encryption keys.

7.1.1.1     Authorization State Machine Overview
A CM begins the authorization process by sending an authentication information message to its CMTS. The
authentication information message contains the device's issuing CA certificate. The authentication information
message is usually informative (i.e., the CMTS may ignore it under some circumstances); however, it does provide a
mechanism for a CM to communicate intermediate CA certificates to the CMTS (see Section 7.1.6.2.5 for more
details).
The CM sends an Authorization Request message to the CMTS. This is a request for an authorization key and for
the SAIDs that identify any static SAs in which the CM is authorized to participate. The CM MUST include the
following common attributes in the Authorization Request message for both BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2.
      •    A description of the security and cryptographic algorithms supported by the CM (see Section 7.2.2.19,
           Security-Capabilities). A CM's cryptographic capabilities are presented to the CMTS as a list of
           cryptographic suite identifiers, each indicating a particular pairing of packet data encryption and packet
           data authentication algorithms, supported by the CM.
      •    The initialization SAID (see Section 7.2.2.12, SAID). The CM MUST use a value of zero for the
           initialization SAID. The CMTS MUST interpret an SAID value of zero as an initialization SAID.
      •    Authorization Request messages additionally include several BPI+ version-specific fields.




36                                                    CableLabs®                                               09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                  CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


When BPI+ V1 is negotiated (see Section 7.1.3, BPI+ Versioning), the CM MUST additionally include the
following fields:
    •    the CM's identification information (see Section 7.2.2.5, CM-Identification) and
    •    a CM device certificate (see Section 7.2.2.18, CM-Certificate) binding the CM's public key to its other
         identifying information. This means that, when using BPI+ V1, the CM public key appears twice in the
         message, once as a standalone entity and once as part of the certificate.
When BPI+ V2 is negotiated (see Section 7.1.3, BPI+ Versioning), the CM MUST additionally include the
following fields.
    •    The BPI+ version (2) (see Section 7.2.2.22, BPI-Version).
    •    The CM's serial number (see Section 7.2.2.1, Serial-Number).
    •    The CM's manufacturer's ID (see Section 7.2.2.2, Manufacturer-ID).
    •    A NONCE value (see Section 7.2.2.31, CM-Nonce).
         The NONCE value is a randomly generated value that is provided by the CM to make sure that replies from
         the CMTS are part of the same authorization exchange (and not replayed from a previous exchange).
    •    A Key Exchange Share (see Section 7.2.2.33, Key-Exchange-Share).
    •    A Message Signature attribute (see Section 7.2.2.32, Message-Signature).
         In BPI+ V2, Authorization Request messages are authenticated by using a detached CMS signature that is
         calculated over all the attributes present in the message in the order they appear in the message definition
         (excluding the signature attribute itself).
The CM MUST include its own ephemeral elliptic curve Diffie-Hellman parameters (ECDHE) to enable mutual key
derivation and perfect forward security.
The CM enables authentication of the Authorization Request message by generating a digital signature with its own
device certificate as described in 7.1.1.2.3.
Upon receiving an Authorization Request message, the CMTS MUST
    •    check the BPI+ version number,
    •    verify the Message-Signature attribute (BPI+ Version 2 only),
    •    validate the CM device certificate and its certificate chain,
    •    check that the CMTS and the CM share at least one cryptographic suite, and
    •    assign the CM's primary SAID.
In addition, the CMTS MUST also verify that the MAC-address attribute of the Auth Request (for BPI+ V1 only),
the MAC address in the CM's device certificate (BPI+ V2), and the source MAC address (BPI+ V1 and V2) of the
Authorization Request message all match.
In BPI+ V2, Authorization Request messages are authenticated using a detached CMS signature that contains the
CM device certificate.
When using BPI+ V2, the CMTS MUST verify the authenticity of the Authorization Request message before
accepting the request as described in Section 7.1.1.2.4, Validation of CM Messages Authentication.
The CMTS SHOULD, by means that are outside the scope of this specification, determine whether the CM is
authorized to receive service and, if so, whether it is entitled to access services that are served by any static SAs.
If these conditions are met, the CMTS MUST then either create (BPI+ Version 1) or derive (BPI+ Version 2) the
keying material for communicating with the CM.
When using BPI+ V1, the CMTS MUST generate an authorization key for the CM and encrypt it with the CM's
public key.




09/03/25                                             CableLabs®                                                          37
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                       Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


When using BPI+ V2, the CMTS MUST both generate its own key exchange parameters and derive the
authorization key by combining such parameters with the public ones received from the CM.
The CM MUST use its own parameters combined with the CMTS's public ones to derive the same authorization
key.
In BPI+ V2, the CMTS MUST enable authentication of the reply message, i.e., the Authorization Reply or the
Authorization Reject message, by generating the message signature as described in Section 7.1.1.2.1, CMTS
Message Authentication.
If the CMTS is able to authenticate the Authorization Request message from the CM but does not authorize the CM
for operation, the CMTS MUST send an Authorization Reject message to the CM containing the following
attributes:
     •   an error code, indicating the reason the CMTS is rejecting the CM (see Section 7.2.2.15, Error-Code) and
     •   a display string, which can include additional human-readable details about the rejection reason.
When BPI+ V2 is negotiated and not authorized, the CMTS MUST additionally include these attributes in the
Authorization Reject message (see Section 7.2.1.3, Authorization Reject (Auth Reject)):
     •   the BPI+ version (see Section 7.2.2.22, BPI-Version),
     •   the NONCE value provided by the CM in the Authorization Request (see Section 7.2.2.31, CM-Nonce),
     •   an optional list of OCSP responses for the CMTS certificate chain (see Section 7.2.2.35, OCSP-Responses),
         and
     •   a message signature together with the corresponding signing CMTS device and issuer certificates (see
         Section 7.2.2.32, Message-Signature).
The CMTS includes the OCSP-Responses attribute (see Section 7.2.2.35, OCSP-Responses) to convey the
revocation status for the CMTS certificate and its issuing CA.
If the CMTS supports static SAs, then the CMTS MUST include the identities of all static SAs associated with the
CM, in addition to the primary SA, in the CMS signature.
The CMTS MUST NOT identify any dynamic SAs (see Section 7.1.2, Encrypted Multicast) in the Authorization
Reject.
If the CMTS authorizes the CM for operation, the CMTS MUST send an Authorization Reply message to the CM
containing the following common attributes (BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2):
     •   a four (4) bit key sequence number (see Section 7.2.2.10 Key-Sequence-Number),
     •   a key lifetime (see Section 7.2.2.9 Key-Lifetime), and
     •   the identities (i.e., the SAIDs) and properties of the primary SA and zero or more static SAs for which the
         CM is authorized to obtain keying information (see Section 7.2.2.23 SA-Descriptor)
If BPI+ V1 is negotiated, the Authorization Reply message, in addition to the common attributes, additionally
contains:
     •   The authorization key encrypted with the CM's public key (see Section 7.2.2.7, Auth-Key).
If BPI+ V2 is negotiated, the Authorization Reply message additionally includes:
     •   the BPI+ version (2), (see Section 7.2.2.22, BPI-Version),
     •   an optional specification of the BPI+ version restrictions (see Section 7.2.2.34, Allowed-BPI-Versions),
     •   the NONCE value provided by the CM in the Authorization Request, (see Section 7.2.2.31, CM-Nonce),
     •   an optional specification of the CMTS designation (see Section 7.2.2.36, CMTS-Designation),
     •   an optional list of OCSP responses for the CMTS certificate chain (see Section 7.2.2.35, OCSP-Responses),
     •   the CMTS's key exchange public parameters (see Section 7.2.2.33, Key-Exchange-Share), and
     •   a message signature together with the corresponding signing CMTS device and issuer certificates (see
         Section 7.2.2.32, Message-Signature).



38                                                 CableLabs®                                                09/03/25
Security Specification                                                               CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


If the CMTS supports static SAs, then the CMTS MUST include in the Authorization Reply message the identities
of all static SAs associated with the CM, in addition to the primary SA.
The CMTS MUST NOT identify any dynamic SAs (see Section 7.1.2, Encrypted Multicast) in the Authorization
Reply message.
NOTE WELL: BPI+ V2 introduces a new method for generating the authorization key. Specifically, ephemeral key
           exchange parameters are used in the authorization message exchange to enable the CMTS and the
           CM to derive the authorization key instead of directly encrypting it for the CM via the
           rsaEncryption field. The new mechanism enables perfect forward secrecy (PFS) and algorithm
           agility.
To generate the signature, the CMTS follows exactly the same procedures used to generate for the Authorization
Reject message signature as described in Section 7.1.1.2.1.
The CMTS includes the OCSP-Responses attribute (see Section 7.2.2.35) to convey the revocation status for the
CMTS certificate and its issuing CA.
The CMTS MAY include the Allowed-BPI-Versions (see Section 7.2.2.34, Allowed-BPI-Versions) and CMTS-
Designation (see Section 7.2.2.36, CMTS-Designation) attributes to specify connection parameters (i.e., the allowed
BPI+ versions and CMTS's certificate filtering) that the CM is required to validate honor during future authorization
exchange(s).
Upon receiving an Authorization Reply, the CM MUST verify the Message-Signature, validate the signer's
certificate chain, and check the CMTS-Designations values (see Section 7.1.1.2.2, Validation of CMTS Messages
Authentication).
The CM MUST discard any Authorization Reply messages that fail signature verification.
If the CM successfully validates all the elements of the Authorization Reply message, the CM MUST store the
Allowed-BPI-Versions and CMTS-Designation attributes from the Authorization Reply message in the
persistent security attributes, if/when present. In BPI+ V2 only, the CM sends the result code of the CMTS
authentication back to the CMTS in an Authentication Status Info message, which enables the CMTS to detect any
authentication issues that might have occurred on the CM.
NOTE WELL: When enabling EAE, the Authentication Status Info message provides the means for the CM to return
           any authentication issue that might happen, thus enabling the possibility to early detect authentication
           and authorization issues.
The CM MUST start a separate TEK state machine for each of the SAIDs identified in the Authorization Reply
message (see Section 7.2.1.2, Authorization Reply (Auth Reply)). Dynamic SAs are not included in the
Authorization Reply and, therefore, are not shown in Figure 8.
If the CM processes a BPI+ V2 Authorization Reply message, the CM MUST issue an Authentication Status Info
message (see Section 7.2.1.13, Authentication Status Info (Auth Status Info)), which includes the following fields
attributes:
    •    the BPI+ version (see Section 7.2.2.22, BPI-Version),
    •    a NONCE value (see Section 7.2.2.31, CM-Nonce) which is a randomly generated value that is provided by
         the CM to make sure that replies from the CMTS are part of the same authorization exchange and not
         replayed from a previous exchange,
    •    a status code indicating the CM's authorization status (see Section 7.2.2.37, CM-Status-Code),
    •    an optional display string providing a human-readable authorization status (see Section 7.2.2.6, Display-
         String),
    •    an optional numerical count of the number of detected authentication issues (see Section 7.2.2.38,
         Detected-Errors), and
    •    a message signature attribute (see Section 7.2.2.32, Message-Signature).
The CM MUST indicate either authentication success or the primary reason for authentication failure using the CM-
Status-Code attribute (see Section 7.2.2.37, CM-Status-Code).



09/03/25                                           CableLabs®                                                        39
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                         Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


The CM SHOULD additionally include the Display-String attribute (see Section 7.2.2.6, Display-String) with
additional human-readable details.
The CM SHOULD detect as many authentication issues as possible and provide a count of any additional issues in
the Detected-Errors attribute (see Section 7.2.2.38, Detected-Errors).
The CM MUST calculate the Message-Signature (see Section 7.2.2.32, Message-Signature) over the BPKM
message header (starting with the BPKM code field) as described in Section 7.1.1.2.3, CM Message Authentication.
Upon receiving an Authentication Status Info message, the CMTS MUST first validate the Message-Signature
as described in Section 7.1.1.2.4, Validation of CM Messages Authentication.
If the Authentication Status Info message is successfully validated, the CMTS can either report the CM's
authorization status to the operator to indicate successful authentication by the CM or to diagnose authentication
issues if/when they occur.

7.1.1.2     Authorization Messages Authentication Overview

7.1.1.2.1      CMTS Message Authentication
When message authentication is needed, e.g., in either the Authorization Reply message or the Authorization Reject
messages, the CMTS MUST generate a Message-Signature attribute and include it in the authenticated message
itself.
The CMTS MUST calculate the Message-Signature attribute (see Section 7.2.2.32, Message-Signature) over the
BPKM message header starting with the BPKM code field and all of the attributes in the message, other than the
message signature itself, in the same order as they appear in the message and place the message signature at the end
of the attribute list.
The CMTS MUST use the private key associated with its device certificate to calculate the signature.
The CMTS MUST include its own certificate in the certificates field of the CMS SignedData structure.
The CMTS MUST include its own device issuing CA certificate in the certificates field of the CMS SignedData
structure.
The CMTS MUST include the OCSP responses in the OCSP-Responses attribute of the message to provide the
revocation status of its own certificate and certificate chain (see Section 7.2.2.35, OCSP-Responses).
The CMTS MAY omit the OCSP responses for certificates in its chain if and only if the URL of the OCSP
responder is not present in the certificate's Authority Info Access extension of the certificate.

7.1.1.2.2      Validation of CMTS Messages Authentication
Upon receiving an authenticated message from the CMTS (e.g., an Authorization Reply or Authorization Reject), the
CM verifies the Message-Signature first.
Specifically, the CM MUST verify the signature over the BPKM message header starting with the BPKM code field
and all the attributes in the message, other than the message signature itself, in the same order as they appear in the
message.
After verifying the message signature, the CM MUST then validate the CMTS certificate chain included in the message
signature and reject messages if the certificate chain is not trusted, i.e., no certificates in the chain are considered as
trusted on the CM.
The CM MUST follow standard basic path validation rules defined in [RFC 5280].
Once time-of-day has been acquired, the CM MUST perform the checks on the validity periods of the certificates and
revocation information that were not performed during BPI+ due to the lack of current time.
In case the certificate chain validation fails, the CM resets the TEK state machine and transitions the Authorization
state machine to the [Silent] state by issuing and logging a CMTS Reject event (see [DOCSIS CM-OSSIv4.0],
Annex C).



40                                                    CableLabs®                                                09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                  CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


The CM MUST check for the presence of the id-cl-pki-ext-eku-CMTS OID in the extended key usage (EKU)
in the CMTS certificate (see Appendix III).
If either the EKU extension is not present in the CMTS certificate or the id-cl-pki-ext-eku-CMTS OID is not
present among the EKU values, the CM MUST reject the message as not trusted.
The CM MUST discard any Authorization Reply messages that fail signature verification.
If the OCSP-Responses attribute is present, the CM MUST validate the revocation status of the CMTS certificate and
its chain by utilizing the OCSP-Responses attribute and rejecting the message if any of the certificate present in the
chain has been revoked.
If the URL of the authoritative OCSP responder is not present in the CMTS certificate or in the CMTS CA certificate,
the CM MAY omit checking the OCSP responses for that certificate.
The CM MUST fail the validation of the CMTS certificate if any of the OCSP-Responses for the CMTS certificate
chain cannot be successfully authenticated or correctly processed.
The CM MUST validate the CMTS certificate (and/or CMTS's issuing CA certificate) meets the criteria of the
CMTS-Designation when specific configurations are present in the persistent security attribute (see Section 12.2,
Persistent Security Attribute Values).
The CM MUST discard any signed messages that fail these validation steps.

7.1.1.2.3     CM Message Authentication
When message authentication is needed (e.g., in Authorization Request or Authentication Status Info messages), the
CM MUST generate a Message-Signature attribute and include it in the authenticated message itself.
The CM MUST calculate the Message-Signature (see Section 7.2.2.32, Message-Signature) over the BPKM
message header starting with the BPKM code field and all of the attributes in the message, other than the message
signature itself, in the same order as they appear in the message and place the signature at the end of the attribute
list.
The CM MUST use the private key associated with its device certificate to calculate the signature.
The CM MUST add its own certificate to the certificates field of the CMS SignedData structure.
The CM MAY add its own issuer CA's certificate to the certificates field of the CMS SignedData structure.

7.1.1.2.4     Validation of CM Messages Authentication
Upon receiving an authenticated message (e.g., a BPI+ V2 Authorization Request message), the CMTS verifies the
Message-Signature first.
The CMTS MUST verify the Message-Signature over the BPKM message header starting with the BPKM code
field and all the attributes in the message, other than the message signature itself, in the same order as they appear in
the message.
The CMTS MUST check for the presence of the id-cl-pki-ext-eku-CM OID in the extended key usage (EKU) in
the CM certificate (see Appendix III).
If either the EKU extension is not present in the CM certificate or the id-cl-pki-ext-eku-CM OID is not present
among the EKU values, the CMTS MUST reject the message as not trusted.
In case the URL of the authoritative OCSP responder is available in the CM device certificate and/or in the CA
certificate or via configuration options, the CMTS MUST query the specific OCSP responder and check the
revocation status of the CM device certificate and its certification chain by following the procedures identified in
CM Validation of CMTS and CA Certificates.
The CMTS MUST discard any signed messages that fail these validation steps.
See Section 13.5 for more information on CRLs and OCSP processing.




09/03/25                                             CableLabs®                                                         41
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                           Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


7.1.1.3     TEK State Machine Overview


                                                    Authorization State
                                                         Machine




          TEK State Machine
           ( primary SAID)
                                   TEK State Machine
                                     (static SAID #1)
                                                             TEK State Machine
                                                               (static SAID #2)
                                                                                   ...      TEK State Machine
                                                                                              ( static SAID #n)



                       Figure 8 - Relationship Among Authorization and TEK State Machines


Each TEK state machine is responsible for managing the keying material associated with its respective SAID. TEK
state machines send Key Request messages to the CMTS, requesting initial and subsequent keying material for their
respective SAIDs. A Key Request contains
     •     identifying information unique to the CM. When BPI+ V1 is used, the identifying information includes the
           manufacturer ID, serial number, MAC address, and the RSA public key. When BPI+ V2 is used, the
           identifying information includes the CM's MAC address;
     •     the SAID whose keying material is being requested; and
     •     an HMAC keyed message digest, authenticating the Key Request.

The CMTS checks the HMAC digest of the Key Request message (see Sections 11 and 12). If the CMTS verifies the
HMAC, then the CMTS MUST respond with a Key Reply message containing the CMTS's active keying material
for the specific SAID. This keying material includes
     •     the 3DES-encrypted TEK,
     •     a CBC initialization vector,
     •     the key sequence number,
     •     the key's remaining lifetime, and
     •     an HMAC keyed message digest.

The TEK in the Key Reply is 3DES (EDE mode) encrypted, using a two-key, 3DES KEK derived from the
authorization key (see Section 11.2).
At all times, the CMTS maintains two active sets (called "generations") of keying material for each SAID. A CMTS
includes in its Key Replies both of an SAID's currently valid generations of keying material.
In addition to the TEK and CBC initialization vector, the Key Reply contains the remaining lifetime of each of the
two sets of keying material. The CM uses these remaining lifetimes to estimate when to schedule Key Requests,
such that the CM requests and receives new keying material before the CMTS invalidates the keying material
currently held by the CM.
The operation of the TEK state machine's Key Request scheduling algorithm, combined with the CMTS's process
for updating and using an SAID's keying material, ensures that the CM will be able at all times to exchange
encrypted traffic with the CMTS.
Before the current authorization key expires, the CM obtains a new authorization key by issuing an Authorization
Request to the CMTS. This "reauthorization" is identical to authorization, with the exception that the CM does not
send an Authentication Information message. The specification of the authorization state machine defines when
Authentication Information messages are sent (see Section 7.1.6).


42                                                      CableLabs®                                            09/03/25
Security Specification                                                               CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


To avoid service interruptions during reauthorization, successive generations of the CM's authorization keys have
overlapping lifetimes.
The CM MUST be able to support at least two simultaneously active authorization keys.
The CMTS MUST be able to support at least two simultaneously active authorization keys for each registered CM.
The operation of the authorization state machine's Authorization Request scheduling algorithm, combined with the
CMTS's process for updating and using a CM's authorization keys, ensures that CMs will be able to refresh TEK
keying information without interruption over the course of the CM's reauthorization periods.
A TEK state machine remains active as long as
    •    the CM has a valid authorization key and
    •    the CMTS continues to provide fresh keying material during TEK re-key cycles.
The parent authorization state machine stops all its child TEK state machines (see Figure 8) when the CM receives
an Authorization Reject during a reauthorization cycle. Individual TEK state machines can be started or stopped
during a reauthorization cycle if a CM's static SAID authorizations change between successive reauthorizations.
Communication between authorization and TEK state machines occurs through the passing of events (directly or
indirectly) between the two state machines. The authorization state machine generates events ({Stop}, {Authorized},
{Authorization Pending}, and {Authorization Complete}) that are sent directly to all its child TEK state machines.
TEK state machines, however, cannot directly send events to their parent authorization state machine.
A TEK state machine affects its parent authorization state machine indirectly through the messaging a CMTS sends
in response to a CM's requests: a CMTS may respond to a TEK machine's Key Requests with a failure response (i.e.,
an Authorization Invalid message) that will be handled by the authorization state machine. In other words, the TEK
state machine might transmit a Key Request to the CMTS, which may respond with an Authentication Invalid
message. This message is handled not by the TEK state machine responsible for transmitting the Key Request but by
its parent authorization state machine.

7.1.2    Encrypted Multicast
The message exchange between the CMTS and the CM for the signaling and initialization of multicast traffic
encryption is dependent on the type of multicast session, on the capabilities of the modem, and on the multicast
forwarding mode selected by the CMTS. The CMTS selects a multicast forwarding mode within the multicast
forwarding capabilities reported by the CM.
Multicast sessions can be established dynamically when a multicast client sends a join request message (IGMP for
IPv4 and MLD for IPv6). Such multicast sessions are called dynamically joined multicast sessions. The cable
operator can also configure the cable modem to join multicast sessions during registration. Such multicast sessions
are called static multicast sessions [DOCSIS MULPIv4.0].
A CM indicates support for downstream service identifier (DSID) multicast forwarding in the Registration Request
message with a multicast DSID forwarding (MDF) capability encoding. A CM that reports in this encoding the
value of either 1 (GMAC-Explicit) or 2 (GMAC-Promiscuous) is said to support MDF. A CM that omits this
encoding or that reports in it a value of 0 (no support for multicast DSID forwarding) is said not to support MDF.
The CMTS enables MDF at a CM that supports MDF by setting the MDF capability encoding to the value 1 or 2 in
the Registration Response it sends to the modem; such a CM is said to be an MDF-enabled CM. The CMTS may
disable MDF at a CM that supports MDF by setting the MDF capability encoding to the value 0 in the Registration
Response it sends to the modem [DOCSIS MULPIv4.0]; such a CM is said to be an MDF-disabled CM.
SAs are used to support encrypted multicast sessions. SA descriptors containing the SAID, the SA type, and
cryptographic suite for encrypted multicast sessions are included in Auth Reply, REG-RSP(-MP), and DBC-REQ
MAC messages and are used by the CM to create or delete the corresponding TEK state machines (see [DOCSIS
MULPIv4.0]). The CMTS typically communicates in REG-RSP dynamic SAs associated with static multicast
sessions. The CMTS typically communicates in DBC-REQ dynamic SAs associated with multicast sessions that are
explicitly joined via multicast management protocols such as IGMP/MLD. Note that a REG-RSP may both enable
DSID multicast forwarding and include added SAs.




09/03/25                                            CableLabs®                                                      43
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                    Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


7.1.2.1     Signaling of Dynamic and Static Multicast Session SAs when MDF is Disabled
If a CM does not support multicast DSID forwarding, then the CMTS MUST NOT signal dynamic SAs to the CM in a
REG-RSP or DBC-REQ message. For CMs that do not support MDF, the CMTS MUST signal SAs using the DOCSIS
1.1/2.0 dynamic security association mechanism described in Annex C, DOCSIS 1.1/2.0 Dynamic Security
Associations (Normative).
If the CMTS disables MDF for a CM that supports MDF, then the CMTS MUST NOT signal SAs used for encrypted
multicast sessions using DBC-REQ to this CM.
The CMTS may signal SAs for other purposes to MDF-disabled CMs using DBC-REQ or REG-RSP(-MP).
The CM MUST accept SA descriptor encodings in REG-RSP(-MP), even if this message disables MDF.
If the CMTS disables MDF on a CM that supports MDF, then the CMTS MUST signal SAs for encrypting IP
multicast traffic to this CM using the DOCSIS 1.1/2.0 dynamic security association mechanism described in Annex
C, DOCSIS 1.1/2.0 Dynamic Security Associations (Normative).
Note that a CMTS may communicate static SAs to an MDF-disabled CM in the BPI+ Auth Reply. Such SAs may be
used for encrypting static multicast sessions. The CM accepts static SAs in BPI+ Auth Reply messages, even when
it operates in MDF-disabled mode.

7.1.2.2     Signaling of Dynamic and Static Multicast Session SAs when MDF is Enabled
When the CMTS enables multicast DSID forwarding for a CM in the REG-RSP(-MP) [DOCSIS MULPIv4.0], the
CM MUST NOT transmit dynamic SA MAP requests to the CMTS. The CMTS MUST respond with an SA Map
Reject message containing error code 7 if it receives an SA Map Request from an MDF-enabled CM.
A CMTS MAY signal in a DBC-REQ the deletion of dynamic SAs known to the CM. When the MDF-enabled CM
receives a DBC-REQ that deletes a dynamic SA, the CM MUST terminate the corresponding TEK state machine
prior to sending the DBC-RSP and remove the dynamic SA's keying material from the CM's key table.
The CM MUST discontinue decryption on an SA deleted through a DBC-REQ message. A CM MUST indicate an
error response to an attempt to delete an unknown SA.
For encrypting multicast sessions (static or dynamic) forwarded through the MDF-enabled CM, the CMTS MUST
use SAs ONLY of type "Dynamic".
The CMTS MUST NOT signal SAs for multicast sessions in BPI Auth Reply messages to an MDF-enabled CM.
The CMTS is allowed to signal SAs in BPI Auth Reply messages to an MDF-enabled CM for purposes other than
multicast encryption. The CM accepts static SAs in BPI+ Auth Reply messages, even when it operates in MDF-
enabled mode.

7.1.2.2.1     Requirements Specific to the Signaling of Dynamic SAs for Dynamic Multicast Sessions
SA descriptors for encrypted multicast sessions joined dynamically are communicated to the MDF-enabled CM in a
DBC-REQ message.
If a dynamic multicast session is encrypted, then the CMTS MUST communicate in a DBC-REQ message the session
SA descriptor to an MDF-enabled CM.
The CMTS MUST set the SA type in the DBC-REQ message to "Dynamic" for a dynamic multicast session.
A CM for which the CMTS has enabled DSID multicast forwarding MUST accept in a DBC-REQ one or more
security association encodings that add a new SA of type "Dynamic".
The CMTS MUST NOT send a dynamic SA in DBC-REQ messages if BPI+ is disabled for a CM.
When an authorized CM receives a DBC-REQ that contains a dynamic SA, the CM MUST start a TEK state
machine for the dynamic SA prior to sending a DBC-RSP message.
The CMTS MUST NOT send a DBC-REQ with a dynamic SA to a CM that is not in the "Authorized" state.




44                                               CableLabs®                                           09/03/25
Security Specification                                                               CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


If an unauthorized CM receives a DBC-REQ message that adds a dynamic SA, then the CM MUST reject the DBC-
REQ message.
The CMTS is allowed to send a DBC-REQ with an SA that employs a cryptographic suite unsupported by the CM.
If an authorized CM receives a DBC-REQ message that adds a dynamic SA having an unsupported cryptographic
suite, then the CM MUST reject the DBC-REQ message.
If a CM receives a DBC-REQ message adding a dynamic SA, in which the TEK state machine for that dynamic SA
is already active, then the CM MUST reject the DBC-REQ message.

7.1.2.2.2     Requirements Specific to the Signaling of Dynamic SAs for Static Multicast Sessions
SA descriptors for encrypted multicast sessions joined statically are communicated to the MDF-enabled CM in the
REG-RSP(-MP) message.
If the static multicast session is encrypted, then the CMTS MUST communicate in REG-RSP(-MP) the session SA
descriptor to an MDF-enabled CM.
The CMTS MUST set the SA type in the REG-RSP(-MP) message to "Dynamic" for a static multicast session.
A CM for which the CMTS has enabled DSID multicast forwarding MUST accept in a REG-RSP(-MP) one or more
security association encodings that add a new SA of type "Dynamic".
The CMTS MUST NOT send a dynamic SA in REG-RSP(-MP) if BPI+ is disabled for a CM.
When an authorized CM receives a REG-RSP(-MP) that adds a dynamic SA, the CM MUST start a TEK state
machine for that dynamic SA.
When an unauthorized CM receives a REG-RSP(-MP) that adds a dynamic SA, the CM MUST wait until it reaches
its Authorized state before starting a TEK state machine for that dynamic SA.
The CMTS is allowed to send a REG-RSP(-MP) with an SA that employs a cryptographic suite unsupported by the
CM.
If the CM receives a REG-RSP(-MP) message that adds a dynamic SA having an unsupported cryptographic suite,
then the CM MUST reject the REG-RSP(-MP) message.

7.1.3    BPI+ Versioning
A CM MUST be able to operate with a CMTS operating with BPI+ V1 and/or BPI+ V2, subject to the configuration
of the persistent security attributes (see Section 12.2, Persistent Security Attribute Values). The CM MUST attempt
to determine the BPI versions and their configurations (i.e., features) that are enabled on the CMTS by inspecting
the MAC Domain Descriptor (MDD) message TLV type 23 which is carried on all primary-capable channels of a
downstream service group. See [DOCSIS MULPIv4.0].
If TLV 23 is not present in the MDD message, a CM MUST assume the CMTS only supports BPI+ V1.

7.1.4    Trust On First Use (TOFU)
With the introduction of multiple versions of BPI+, there is the potential risk for unauthorized downgrades from one
version of BPI+ to another. Specifically, the CM is required to keep track of successful authentication configuration
and use that information to prevent using older versions of BPI+ unless explicitly allowed to do so by the CMTS and
only after a successful authentication. Specifically, the trust on first use (TOFU) approach provides protection
against unauthorized downgrades by allowing CMs to trust the BPI+ parameters used during the first connection to a
CMTS and then check that the same parameters (or better) are used in subsequent connections.
The primary element of TOFU is the set of persistent security attribute (PSA) values (see Section 12.2 for details)
that are stored on the CM. PSA values have the following characteristics:
    1.   The initial default PSA values allow a CM to authenticate with the CMTS by using any supported BPI+
         versions. A CM where no previous PSA values are stored (e.g., after a factory reset) MUST allow for any
         version of BPI+ to be used during the authorization process.




09/03/25                                           CableLabs®                                                         45
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                         Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


     2.   After a successful authentication, the CM PSA values are updated to the used version of the protocol unless
          the CMTS indicates that earlier versions of the protocol are still allowed by including the Allowed-BPI-
          Versions attribute TLV in the Authorization Reply message or by directly configuring CM PSA values via
          an authenticated management interface. For example, a CM that authenticates with BPI+ V2 can have its
          PSA values configured to still allow BPI+ V1 for future authentications in case BPI+ V2 is not available.
          This feature can be used when mixed deployment exists where BPI+ V2 has not been enabled across the
          entire network.
     3.   CM PSA values can be set (or reset) via authenticated management interfaces such as SSH.
     4.   The CMTS can set (or reset) PSA values only when BPI+ V2 is used.
     5.   CM PSA values can be used to restrict CMs to only authenticate with specific CMTSs (i.e., CMTS-
          pinning) and/or enable only specific BPI+ versions.
     6.   CM PSA values can be reset to the initial default values via a physical factory reset of the CM.
This combination of features enable the secure provisioning of CMs while still enabling the relocation of a CM onto
a different CMTS that supports different BPI+ versions and/or present different values in their certificates or their
issuing CA's certificates (see Section 7.1.4.4).
The ability to reset the configuration of PSA values by CM factory reset enables the physical relocation of a CM or
that CM to change service providers without requiring support or intervention from the operator.
When changes in PSA values are needed, the CMTS operator can use the following options to update the PSA
values:
     a.   reset the version and/or certificate attributes stored in the PSA values by forcing a re-authentication of the
          CM before moving the CM to the new CMTS, or
     b.   reset the PSA values via a management interface (e.g., SNMP or SSH) after a successful authentication but
          still before moving the CM to the new CMTS. After the CM successfully authenticates to the new CMTS,
          new PSA values are sent to the CM to bind the CM to the new CMTS for subsequent authentications.
A CM MUST implement the TOFU mechanism as defined in this specification.
A CM MUST reject setting (or resetting) of PSA values when BPI+ V1 is used for authorization.
A CM MUST allow setting (or resetting) of PSA values when BPI+ V2 is used for authentication and authorization.
A CM MUST support factor reset where PSA values are cleared to factory default.

7.1.4.1    Persistent Security Attribute Values
The CM's persistent security attribute (PSA) values are used to persistently store security-related data across CM
initializations and power cycles. PSA values are used to prevent unauthorized downgrades and provide additional
network authentication controls, e.g., CMTS identity pinning.
In order to prevent unauthorized downgrades from one version of BPI+ to another, when the CM issues a new
Authorization Request message, the CM first checks the values of the allowed BPI+ versions to select the latest
supported version of BPI+ among the ones announced by the CMTS (see Section 7.1.3).
PSA values are also used to specify restrictions to the presented CMTS certificate or CMTS CA certificate during
authorization. In particular, when receiving either an Authorization Reply message or an Authorization Reject
message, the CM checks the values configured in the CMTS-Designation attributes against the CMTS
certificate or in the CMTS CA certificate (e.g., set the accepted value(s) for the Organization (O) field in the
certificate subject) and rejects the connection if the values do not match (see Section 7.2.2.36).
The CM MUST retain the values of the PSAs across CM initializations and power cycles.
The CM MUST set the PSA values to the default factory values when no previous configuration exists.
The CMTS MUST allow an operator to set specific values for the Allowed-BPI-Versions attribute to set the
minimum version of BPI+ the CM is allowed to use.




46                                                    CableLabs®                                                09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                 CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


                                   Table 6 - Persistent Security Attribute Values

      Attribute                             Contents                              Default                  See
 Allowed-BPI-Versions    Allowed BPI+ versions for future authorizations               0              Section 7.2.2.34
 CMTS-Designation        CMTS designation parameters                                None              Section 7.2.2.36



7.1.4.2    PSA Value Reset
When the configured PSA values are not supported by the CMTS (e.g., the CM has been moved to a different
location where BPI+ V2 is not enabled), the CM refuses to complete the authorization process until the PSA values
are reset, possibly through a factory reset of the CM or by the operator resetting the PSA values through a
management interface.
To allow PSA values to be cleared, the CM MUST support a method to reset the PSA values that requires physical
access to the device to operate it (i.e., cannot be operated remotely).
For instance, the PSAs reset can be enabled as part of the CM factory reset process.
The CM MUST set the value of the PSAs to the default factory values when the reset mechanism is operated.

7.1.4.3    Enabling Support for Multiple BPI+ Versions
To allow for either planned downgrades or when BPI+ V2 is not enabled on every CMTS, operators need the
possibility to configure the CM to enable BPI+ V1 for future authentications even when the CM successfully
authenticates by using BPI+ V2.
The CMTS can use the Allowed-BPI-Versions in the Authorization Reply message to restrict which versions
(can be more than one) of BPI+ can be enabled on the CM.
The CM MUST store the values carried in the Allowed-BPI-Versions attribute in the Authorization Reply
message in its PSA values only after a successful BPI+ V2 authentication and authorization, i.e., after the CM
successfully verifies the signature over the message contents, validates the identity of the signing CMTS, verifies the
CMTS meets the requirements of the current PSA values, and the CM is able to derive the authorization key as part
of the authorization process (see Section 7.1.1.2, Authorization Messages Authentication Overview).
The CM MUST ignore the values carried in the Allowed-BPI-Versions attribute in the Authorization Reply
message when BPI+ V2 authorization is not successfully completed.
A CM MUST NOT issue authorization messages for BPI+ versions that are not enabled in the configured PSA
values.
A CM MUST ignore authorization messages for BPI+ versions that are not explicitly included in the PSA values.
See Section 7.1.3 for more information on determining the BPI+ version.

7.1.4.4    CMTS Certificate Designation
When additional checks are required to make sure that the CM only connects to specific CMTSs, it is possible to
configure the PSA values to require specific values for the CMTS certificate or the CMTS CA certificate.
The CMTS-Designation attribute in the Authorization Reply message can be used to restrict the identities of which
CMTSs the CM is allowed to authenticate with. The use of CMTS-Designation attributes provides certificate
pinning capabilities for both the CMTS certificate and the CMTS CA certificate.
For instance, to restrict the organization names that CMs will accept during network authentication, the CMTS sets
the acceptable value of the Organization (O) field in the PSA values (e.g., "CableLabs") that will be used in future
authorizations.
A CMTS MUST allow the operator to configure the CMTS-Designation attribute with specific values for either
the elements of the CMTS certificate or the CMTS CA certificate.
A CMTS MUST allow the operator to configure the CMTS-Designation attribute either globally or on per-CM
basis.



09/03/25                                              CableLabs®                                                    47
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                      Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


A CM MUST verify that the values configured via the CMTS-Designation attributes in the PSA values match
the values in either the CMTS certificate or in the CMTS CA certificate (see Section 7.2.2.36, CMTS-Designation).
A CM MUST reject messages from a CMTS when either the CMTS certificate or the CMTS CA certificate
parameters differ from the configured ones in the PSA values.

7.1.5    Selecting Cryptographic Suites
As part of their authorization exchange, the CM provides the CMTS with a list of supported cryptographic suites.
The CMTS selects from this list a single suite to use with the CM's primary SA. In the Authorization Reply, the
CMTS includes a primary SA descriptor that identifies the cryptographic suite selected by the CMTS.
The CMTS MUST reject the Authorization Request if none of the offered cryptographic suites are permitted by
local policy.
The Authorization Reply may contain a list of static SA descriptors; each static SA descriptor identifies the
cryptographic suite employed by that SA. The selection of a static SA's cryptographic suite is independent of the
requesting CM's cryptographic capabilities.
The CMTS MAY include in its Authorization Reply static SA descriptors identifying cryptographic suites
unsupported by the CM.
The CM MUST NOT start TEK state machines for SAs whose cryptographic suites the CM does not support.

7.1.6    Authorization State Machine
The authorization finite state machine (FSM) contains six states and ten events. The authorization FSM is presented
below as a state flow diagram (Figure 9) and as a state transition matrix (Table 7).




                              Figure 9 - Authorization State Machine Flow Diagram




48                                                 CableLabs®                                              09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                          CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


                                          Table 7 - Authorization FSM Transition Matrix
       State Event               [Start]         [Auth Wait]        [Authorized]    [Reauth Wait]           [Auth       [Silent]
  or Received Message                                                                                    Reject Wait]
 {Initiate Authentication}     [Auth Wait]
 {Auth Reject}                                 [Auth Reject Wait]                   [Auth Reject Wait]
 {Perm Auth Reject}                            [Silent]                             [Silent]
 {EAE Disabled Auth Reject}                    [Start]
 {Auth Reply}                                  [Authorized]                         [Authorized]
 {Timeout}                                     [Auth Wait]                          [Reauth Wait]        [Auth Wait]
 {Auth Grace Timeout}                                               [Reauth Wait]
 {Auth Invalid}                                                     [Reauth Wait]   [Reauth Wait]
 {Reauth}                                                           [Reauth Wait]
 {Unsupported BPI+             [Silent]
 Version}
 {CMTS Reject}                                                      [Silent]


The state flow diagram depicts the protocol messages transmitted and internal events generated for each of the
machine's state transitions; however, the diagram does not indicate additional internal actions, such as the clearing or
starting of timers that accompany the specific state transitions. Accompanying the state transition matrix is a detailed
description of the specific actions accompanying each state transition.
The CM MUST use the text associated with the state transition matrix as the definitive specification of protocol
actions associated with each state transition.
The following legend applies to Figure 9.
     •      States are represented by ovals.
     •      Events appear in italics.
     •      Messages appear in normal font.
     •      State transitions (i.e., the lines between states) are labeled in the manner of "<what causes the
            transition>/<messages and events triggered by the transition>." So "timeout/Auth Request" means that the
            state received a "timeout" event and sent an Auth Request message. If there are multiple comma-separated
            events or messages before the slash "/", any of them can cause the transition. If there are multiple events or
            messages listed after the slash, all the identified actions accompany the transition.
The authorization state transition matrix, presented in Table 7, lists the states in the topmost row and the events in
the left-most column. Cells within the matrix represent a specific combination of state and event, with the next state
(the state to which the machine transitioned) displayed within the cell. For example, one cell represents the receipt
of an Authorization Reply message when in the [Auth Wait] state. Within this cell is the name of the next state,
[Authorized]. Thus, when a CM's authorization state machine is in the [Auth Wait] state and an Authorization Reply
message is received, the authorization state machine will transition to the [Authorized] state. In conjunction with this
state transition, several protocol actions are taken; these are described in Section 7.1.6.7.
A shaded cell within the state transition matrix (Table 7) implies that the specific event should not occur within that
state.
If the event does occur, the CM MUST NOT transition to another state. For example, if an Authorization Reply
message arrives when in the [Authorized] state, that message will not cause any state transition to occur.
The CM MAY, in response to such an improper event, log the event's occurrence, generate an SNMP event, or take
some other vendor-defined action.




09/03/25                                                      CableLabs®                                                      49
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                        Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


7.1.6.1     Brief Description of States

7.1.6.1.1      [Start]
This is the initial state of the FSM. No resources are assigned to or used by the FSM, all timers are off, and no
processing is scheduled.

7.1.6.1.2      [Auth Wait]
The CM has received the {Initiate Authentication} event. In response to receiving the event, the CM has sent both
an Authentication Information and an Authorize Request message and is waiting for the reply.

7.1.6.1.3      [Authorized]
The CM has received an Authorization Reply message that contains a list of valid SAIDs for this CM. The CM has a
valid authorization key and the list of SAIDs. Transition into this state triggers the creation of one TEK FSM for
each of the CM's privacy-enabled SAIDs.

7.1.6.1.4      [Reauth Wait]
The CM has an outstanding Reauthorization Request. The CM's current authorization is about to time out, or the CM
has received an indication (an Authorization Invalid message) that its authorization is no longer valid. The CM has
sent an Authorization Request message to the CMTS and is waiting for a response.

7.1.6.1.5      [Auth Reject Wait]
The CM received an Authorization Reject message in response to its last Authorization Request. The error code in
the Authorization Reject indicated that the error was not permanent or that EAE is not disabled. In response to
receiving this reject message, the CM sets a timer and has transitioned to the [Auth Reject Wait] state. The CM
remains in this state until the timer expires.

7.1.6.1.6      [Silent]
The CM received an Authorization Reject message in response to its last Authorization Request and the error code
indicated that the error was permanent or the CM and CMTS fail to properly authenticate. This triggers a transition
to the [Silent] state.
In the [Silent] state, the CM MUST NOT pass CPE traffic.
In the [Silent] state, if the CM has a valid IP address, then the CM MUST respond to SNMP management requests
arriving from the cable network.
The CMTS can send unencrypted data traffic to a CM on an SAID for which it has sent an Authorization Reject
message, or the CMTS can block such traffic.

7.1.6.2     Brief Description of Messages
The corresponding message formats are specified in Section 7.2.

7.1.6.2.1      Authorization Request (Auth Request)
Request an authorization key and a list of authorized SAIDs. The Authorization Request is sent from the CM to the
CMTS.

7.1.6.2.2      Authorization Reply (Auth Reply)
Receive an authorization key or a set of key exchange public parameters, and a list containing the primary SAID and
static SAIDs. The Authorization Reply is sent from the CMTS to the CM. The authorization key is encrypted with
the CM's public key (BPI+ V1) or derived from the combination of the CM parameters and the CMTS parameters
(BPI+ V2).




50                                                  CableLabs®                                                09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                 CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


7.1.6.2.3      Authorization Reject (Auth Reject)
The attempt to authorize was rejected. The Authorization Reject is sent from the CMTS to the CM.

7.1.6.2.4      Authorization Invalid (Auth Invalid)
The CMTS can send an Authorization Invalid message to a client CM as
    •     an unsolicited indication or
    •     a response to a message received from that CM.
In either case, the Authorization Invalid message instructs the receiving CM to reauthorize with its CMTS.
The CMTS MUST respond to a Key Request with an Authorization Invalid message if
    1.    there is no valid authorization key associated with the CM or
    2.    verification of the Key Request's keyed message digest (in the HMAC-Digest attribute) failed.

7.1.6.2.5      Authentication Information (Auth Info)
The Authentication Information message contains the intermediate CA certificate (see Section 7.2.1.9) issued by the
root CA that issued the CM's certificate. The Auth Information message is sent by the CM to the CMTS.
The CMTS MUST first use the out-of-band configuration information to obtain the intermediate CA certificate (see
Section 13.4.2, CMTS Validation of CA Certificates and CM Certificates).
If the CMTS does not learn the intermediate CA certificate from the out-of-band configuration information, then the
CMTS MUST use the intermediate CA certificate from the Auth Info message sent by the CM.

7.1.6.2.6      Authentication Status Info (Auth Status Info)
The Authentication Status Info message is only used in BPI+ V2 and carries the status code for the results of the
CM's authentication of the CMTS credentials to the CMTS itself. The message provides an indication of error(s) that
have occurred on the CM when validating the CMTS's message.

7.1.6.3     Brief Description of Events

7.1.6.3.1      {Initiate Authentication}
The CM sends an {Initiate Authentication} event to the Authorization FSM upon completing CMTS registration or,
if EAE is enabled, when it completes ranging. If the configuration file contains a BPI+ enable setting (see Annex
A.1.1) and the CM has not yet enabled BPI+, then the {Initiate Authentication} event causes the CM to begin the
process of obtaining its authorization key and transitions to the [Auth Wait] state.

7.1.6.3.2      {Timeout}
This event indicates that a retransmission or wait timer timed out. Generally, a request is resent.

7.1.6.3.3      {Auth Grace Timeout}
This event indicates that the Authorization Grace timer fired. This timer fires a configurable duration (the
Authorization Grace Time) before the current authorization expires, signaling the CM to reauthorize before its
authorization actually expires. Once the configuration file has been received by the CM, the Authorization Grace
Time is obtained from a value in that file; prior to that time, the value of the Authorization Grace Time MUST be
the default defined in Annex A.

7.1.6.3.4      {Reauth}
This event is generated in response to an SNMP SET [DOCSIS CM-OSSIv4.0] that is intended to trigger a
reauthorization cycle because the CM's set of authorized static SAIDs may have changed.




09/03/25                                             CableLabs®                                                     51
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                       Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


7.1.6.3.5      {Auth Invalid}
This event is generated when there is a failure authenticating a Key Reply, Key Reject, or TEK Invalid message or
when the CM receives an Authorization Invalid message. A CMTS responds to a Key Request with an
Authorization Invalid message if verification of the request's message authentication code fails. Generation of an
{Auth Invalid} event indicates that the CMTS and the CM have lost authorization key synchronization.
A CMTS MAY send an unsolicited Authorization Invalid message to a CM, forcing an {Auth Invalid} event.

7.1.6.3.6      {Perm Auth Reject}
This event indicates receipt of an Authorization Reject message containing either error code 6 or error code 11.
When a CM receives an Authorization Reject containing one of these error codes, the authorization state machine
moves to the [Silent] state.

7.1.6.3.7      {Auth Reject}
This event indicates that the CM has received an Authorization Reject in response to an Authorization Request and
that the error code in the Authorization Reject had some value other than 6 or 10. The CM's authorization state
machine will set a wait timer and transition into the [Auth Reject Wait] state. The CM remains in this state until the
timer expires, at which time it will re-attempt authorization.

7.1.6.3.8      {Unsupported BPI-Version}
This event indicates that the BPI+ version the CM is attempting to use is not supported by the CMTS. This condition
can be detected
     1.   by the CM when comparing the supported and enabled BPI+ version(s) to the version(s) signaled by the
          CMTS and finding no compatible version or
     2.   by the CMTS receiving an Authorization Request message with an incompatible BPI+ version and issuing
          an Authorization Reject message with error code 11.

7.1.6.3.9      {EAE Disabled Auth Reject}
This event indicates that the CM has received an Authorization Reject message containing error code 10 in response
to an Authorization Request that was sent to the CMTS as part of EAE. Authorization Reject error code 10 messages
are sent to CMs that have been specifically configured in the CMTS to have EAE disabled. When receiving this
message, the CM's authorization state machine will transition to the [Start] state.

7.1.6.3.10     {CMTS Reject}
This event indicates that the CM has rejected the CMTS certificate. This event can occur when delayed time checks
on the CMTS certificate or associated revocation information yield a negative result or when there is a problem with
the certificate chain validation.
The CM MUST log the CMTS Reject event and transition into the [Silent] state.

7.1.6.4     Events Sent to TEK State Machine
The following events are sent by the authorization state machine to a TEK state machine.

7.1.6.4.1      {TEK Stop}
Sent by the authorization FSM to a TEK FSM that is not in the [Start] state to terminate the TEK FSM and remove
the corresponding SAID's keying material.

7.1.6.4.2      {TEK Authorized}
Sent by the authorization FSM to a TEK FSM that is in the [Start] state.




52                                                  CableLabs®                                               09/03/25
Security Specification                                                               CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


7.1.6.4.3      {Auth Pend}
This message is sent by the authorization FSM to a TEK FSM to place that TEK FSM into a wait state until the
authorization FSM can complete a reauthorization operation.

7.1.6.4.4      {Auth Comp}
Sent by the authorization FSM to a TEK FSM in the [Op Reauth Wait] or [Rekey Reauth Wait] states to clear the
wait state begun by an {Auth Pend} event.

7.1.6.5     Brief Description of Timing Parameters
All configuration parameter values are contained in the configuration file (see Annex A).

7.1.6.5.1      Authorize Wait Timeout (Auth Wait Timeout)
This is the timeout period between sending Authorization Request messages when in the [Auth Wait] state.

7.1.6.5.2      Reauthorize Wait Timeout (Reauth Wait Timeout)
This is the timeout period between sending Authorization Request messages when in the [Reauth Wait] state.

7.1.6.5.3      Authorization Grace Time (Auth Grace Timeout)
Amount of time before authorization is scheduled to expire that the CM starts the process of reauthorization.

7.1.6.5.4      Authorize Reject Wait Timeout (Auth Reject Wait Timeout)
Amount of time that a CM's authorization FSM remains in the [Auth Reject Wait] state before re-attempting
authorization.

7.1.6.6     Timers

7.1.6.6.1      Authorization Request
Used when awaiting a response to Authorization Requests.

7.1.6.6.2      Authorization Reject
Used after receipt of an Authorization Reject.

7.1.6.6.3      Authorization Grace
Used when determining when to reauthorize.

7.1.6.7     Actions
The CM MUST take the following actions in association with state transitions.
[Start] + {Initiate Authentication} → [Auth Wait]:
    •     Send Authentication Information message to CMTS;
    •     Send Authorization Request message to CMTS;
    •     Set Authorization Request timer to Authorize Wait Timeout.
[Start] + {Unsupported BPI+ Version} → [Silent]:
    •     Disable all forwarding of CPE traffic.
    •     Send the Authentication Status Info to the CMTS indicating the specific error.




09/03/25                                             CableLabs®                                                 53
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                       Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


[Auth Wait] + {Auth Reject} → [Auth Reject Wait]:
     •   Clear Authorization Request timer;
     •   Set Authorization Reject timer to Authorize Reject Wait Timeout.
[Reauth Wait] + {Auth Reject} → [Auth Reject Wait]:
     •   Clear Authorization Request timer;
     •   Generate TEK FSM {Stop} events for all active TEK state machines;
     •   Set Authorization Reject timer to Authorize Reject Wait Timeout.
[Auth Wait] + {Perm Auth Reject} → [Silent]:
     •   Clear Authorization Request timer;
     •   Disable all forwarding of CPE traffic.
[Auth Wait] + {EAE Disabled Auth Reject} → [Start]:
     •   Clear Authorization Request timer.
[Auth Wait] + {Unsupported BPI+ Version} → [Start]:
     •   Clear Authorization Request timers;
     •   Send the Authentication Status Info to the CMTS indicating the specific error.
[Reauth Wait] + {Perm Auth Reject} → [Silent]:
     •   Clear Authorization Request timer;
     •   Generate TEK FSM {Stop} events for all active TEK state machines;
     •   Disable all forwarding of CPE traffic.
[Auth Wait] + {Auth Reply} → [Authorized]:
     •   (BPI+ V2 only) Validate the authentication information (signature) of the received authorization and send
         an Auth Status Info with the results of the authentication back to the CMTS. If authentication fails, the CM
         discards the received {Auth Reply } message resulting in no state changes;
     •   Clear Authorization Request timer;
     •   Decrypt (BPI+ V1) or derive (BPI+ V2) and record the authorization key delivered with Authorization
         Reply;
     •   Start TEK FSMs for all SAIDs listed in Authorization Reply and any pending dynamic SAIDs received in
         Registration Response (provided that the CM supports the cryptographic suite that is associated with an
         SAID) and issue a TEK FSM {Authorized} event for each of the new TEK FSMs;
     •   Set the Authorization Grace timer to fire "Authorization Grace Time" seconds prior to the supplied
         authorization key's scheduled expiration.
[Reauth Wait] + {Auth Reply} → [Authorized]:
     •   (BPI+ V2 only) Validate the authentication information (signature) of the received authorization and send
         an Auth Status Info with the results of the authentication back to the CMTS. If authentication fails, the CM
         discards the received {Auth Reply } message resulting in no state changes;
     •   Clear Authorization Request timer;
     •   Decrypt (BPI+ V1) or derive (BPI+ V2) and record the authorization key delivered with Authorization
         Reply;




54                                                 CableLabs®                                               09/03/25
Security Specification                                                             CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


    •   Start TEK FSMs for any newly authorized SAIDs listed in Authorization Reply (provided that the CM
        supports the cryptographic suite that is associated with the new SAID) and issue TEK FSM {Authorized}
        events for each of the new TEK FSMs;
    •   Generate TEK FSM {Authorization Complete} events for any currently active TEK FSMs whose
        corresponding SAIDs were listed in Authorization Reply;
    •   Generate TEK FSM {Stop} events for any currently active TEK FSMs whose corresponding primary or
        static SAIDs were not listed in Authorization Reply;
    •   Set the Authorization Grace timer to fire "Authorization Grace Time" seconds prior to the supplied
        authorization key's scheduled expiration.
[Auth Wait] + {Timeout} → [Auth Wait]:
    •   Send Authentication Information message to CMTS;
    •   Send Authorization Request message to CMTS;
    •   Set Authorization Request timer to Authorize Wait Timeout.
[Reauth Wait] + {Timeout} → [Reauth Wait]:
    •   Send Authorization Request message to CMTS;
    •   Set Authorization Request timer to Reauthorize Wait Timeout.
[Auth Reject Wait] + {Timeout} → [Auth Wait]:
    •   Send Authentication Information message to CMTS;
    •   Send Authorization Request message to CMTS;
    •   Set Authorization Request timer to Authorize Wait Timeout.
[Authorized] + {Auth Grace Timeout} → [Reauth Wait]:
    •   Send Authorization Request message to CMTS;
    •   Set Authorization Request timer to Reauthorize Wait Timeout.
[Authorized] + {Auth Invalid} → [Reauth Wait]:
    •   Clear Authorization Grace timer;
    •   Send Authorization Request message to CMTS;
    •   Set Authorization Request timer to Reauthorize Wait Timeout;
    •   If the Authorization Invalid event is associated with a particular TEK FSM, generate a TEK FSM
        {Authorization Pending} event for the TEK state machine responsible for the Authorization Invalid event
        (i.e., the TEK FSM that either generated the event or sent the Key Request message to which the CMTS
        responded with an Authorization Invalid message).
[Reauth Wait] + {Auth Invalid} → [Reauth Wait]:
    •   If the Authorization Invalid event is associated with a particular TEK FSM, generate a TEK FSM
        {Authorization Pending} event for the TEK state machine responsible for the Authorization Invalid event
        (i.e., the TEK FSM that either generated the event or sent the Key Request message to which the CMTS
        responded with an Authorization Invalid message).
[Authorized] + {Reauth} → [Reauth Wait]:
    •   Clear Authorization Grace timer;
    •   Send Authorization Request message to CMTS;
    •   Set Authorization Request timer to Reauthorize Wait Timeout.

CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                                          Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


7.1.7   TEK State Machine
The TEK state machine contains six states and nine events. The TEK state machine is presented as a state flow
diagram in Figure 10 and a state transition matrix in Table 8.
The CM MUST use the state transition matrix, together with the required actions in Section 7.1.7.6, Actions, as the
definitive specification of protocol actions associated with each state transition.
Shaded states in Figure 10 ({Operational}, {Rekey Wait}, and {Rekey Reauthorize Wait}) indicate that the CM
holds valid keying material, so encrypted traffic can be passed.
The authorization state machine starts an independent TEK state machine for each of its authorized SAIDs.
The CMTS maintains two active TEKs per SAID. The CMTS includes in its Key Replies both of these TEKs, along
with their remaining lifetimes. The CMTS encrypts downstream traffic with the older of its two TEKs and decrypts
upstream traffic with either the older or newer TEK, depending on which of the two keys the CM used. The CM
encrypts upstream traffic with the newer of its two TEKs and decrypts downstream traffic with either the older or
newer key, depending on which of the two keys the CMTS used. See Sections 10.1 and 10.2 for details on CMTS
and CM key usage requirements, respectively.
Through operation of a TEK state machine, the CM attempts to keep its copies of an SAID's TEKs, synchronized
with those of its CMTS. A TEK state machine issues Key Requests to refresh copies of its SAID's keying material
after the scheduled expiration time of the older of its two TEKs and before the expiration of its newer TEK. To
accommodate CM/CMTS clock skew and other system processing and transmission delays, the CM schedules its
Key Requests a configurable number of seconds (the TEK Grace Time) before the newer TEK expires.
When it receives a Key Reply, the CM MUST immediately begin to use the TEK parameters from the TEKs
contained in the Key Reply message.
Figure 10 illustrates the CM's scheduling of key refreshes in conjunction with its management of an SA's active
TEKs.

                                                                                                                   Stop /




                                           Start

                               Stop /                                                                  Stop /

               Op Reauth

                                         Key Request     Stop/
                               Auth Pend /
