I.12 Encryption of PDU with Payload Header Suppression (AES)
These examples show how encryption is applied to a PDU when payload header suppression (PHS) is applied. The
examples use an RTP [RFC 3550] voice over IP payload with the Ethernet Type/Length field and the IP and UDP
headers suppressed. In the examples, no effort is made to use correct values for the fields of the PDU. As a result,
the examples here are not valid packets suitable for transmission. The intent of the examples is to illustrate
encryption details only.

I.12.1 Downstream

Suppose that the PDU, after PHS and prior to encryption, is as follows.
DA                01 02 03 04 05 06
SA                f1 f2 f3 f4 f5 f6
RTP header        21 22 23 24 25 26 27 28 29 2a 2b 2c
Voice data        31 32 33 34 35 36 37 38 39 3a
CRC               93 86 b3 b9


PHS has removed the Type/Len field that would otherwise be included in the Ethernet/802.3 header. The user data
consists of the RTP header and the voice data. Encryption is applied beginning with the first octet of the RTP header
and ending with the last octet of the CRC, as follows.
Mode              AES-CBC
Key               e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a 81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a
Plaintext         21 22 23 24 25 26 27 28
                  29 2a 2b 2c 31 32 33 34
Ciphertext        b6 d6 90 a5 8e 75 1d 00
                  9e 70 4f 1f 76 b1 5d 88


Mode              AES-CFB128
Key               e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                b6 d6 90 a5 8e 75 1d 00
                  9e 70 4f 1f 76 b1 5d 88
Plaintext         35 36 37 38 39 3a 93 86 b3 b9 00 00 00 00 00 00
Ciphertext        26 25 03 a4 e5 01 d7 ab d9 9e 9a 63 00 22 44 36


The PDU, after encryption, looks like the following.
DA                01 02 03 04 05 06
SA                f1 f2 f3 f4 f5 f6
RTP header        b6 d6 90 a5 8e 75 1d 00
                  9e 70 4f 1f
Voice data        76 b1 5d 88 af 4f a8 b4 ba 8a
CRC               6f 17 6b 7a




09/03/25                                           CableLabs®                                                    225
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                       Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


I.12.2 Upstream

Suppose that the PDU, after PHS and prior to encryption, is as follows.
RTP header         21 22 23 24 25 26 27 28 29 2a 2b 2c
Voice data         31 32 33 34 35 36 37 38 39 3a 3b 3c 3d 3e
CRC                65 cf fe 89

PHS has removed the DA, SA, and Type/Len fields that would otherwise be included in the Ethernet/802.3 header
as well as the IP and UDP headers. The user data consists of the RTP header and the voice data. The first 12 octets
of the user data are not encrypted.
Encryption is applied beginning with the first octet of the voice data and ending with the last octet of the CRC, as
follows.
Mode               AES-CBC
Key                e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                 81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a 81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a
Plaintext          31 32 33 34 35 36 37 38 39 3a 3b 3c 3d 3e 65 cf
Ciphertext         ce 8c e2 55 1c e3 d2 6c 3f 06 f6 e9 66 e7 f7 d3


Mode               AES-CFB128
Key                e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                 ce 8c e2 55 1c e3 d2 6c 3f 06 f6 e9 66 e7 f7 d3
Plaintext          fe 89 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00
Ciphertext         4e 4e 1f c5 36 37 3e d7 bc 82 ad 52 8e 6c 05 dd


The PDU, after encryption, looks like the following.
RTP header         21 22 23 24 25 26 27 28 29 2a 2b 2c
Voice data         ce 8c e2 55 1c e3 d2 6c 3f 06 f6 e9 66 e7
CRC                f7 d3 4e 4e




226                                                 CableLabs®                                               09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                  CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


I.13 Fragmented Packet Encryption (AES)
When a packet is fragmented, each fragment is independently encrypted using CBC mode with residual block
processing. The TEK and IV for each fragment are the same TEK and IV that are used for encrypting an
unfragmented PDU. All octets of a fragment are encrypted, including the 12 octets carrying the Ethernet/802.3
destination and source addresses (DA/SA) of the packet PDU.
In the example here, no effort is made to use meaningful values for the fields of the packet. As a result, the example
here is not a valid packet suitable for transmission. The intent of the example is to illustrate encryption details only.
In this example, the TEK and IV are taken from the example Key Reply packet described above.
Suppose that packet is divided into two fragments, as follows.
Fragment 1 payload                01 02 03 04 05 06 f1 f2 f3 f4 f5 f6 00 01 02 03 04 05
Fragment 1 CRC                    b4 2b 6d d4


Fragment 2 payload                06 07 08 09 0a 0b 0c 0d 06 07 08 09 0a 0b 0c 0d
Fragment 2 CRC                    48 34 45 36


The first fragment is encrypted using AES-CBC and AES-CFB128, as follows.
Mode               AES-CBC
Key                e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                 81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a 81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a
Plaintext          01 02 03 04 05 06 f1 f2 f3 f4 f5 f6 00 01 02 03
Ciphertext         63 48 92 62 01 a9 88 08 df a3 55 30 7b 99 65 1e


Mode               AES-CFB128
Key                e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                 63 48 92 62 01 a9 88 08 df a3 55 30 7b 99 65 1e
Plaintext          04 05 b4 2b 6d d4 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00
Ciphertext         e9 ad be e7 be d5 88 9d 35 ff 76 ce 29 26 98 04


The first fragment, after encryption, looks like the following.
Fragment 1 payload                63 48 92 62 01 a9 88 08 df a3 55 30 7b 99 65 1e e9 ad
Fragment 1 CRC                    be e7 be d5


The second fragment is encrypted using AES-CBC and AES-CFB128, as follows.
Mode               AES-CBC
Key                e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                 81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a 81 0e 52 8e 1c 5f da 1a
Plaintext          06 07 08 09 0a 0b 0c 0d 06 07 08 09 0a 0b 0c 0d
Ciphertext         62 f0 15 15 b4 18 d5 55 f7 52 5e 42 51 0b 77 e8




09/03/25                                             CableLabs®                                                      227
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                      Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


Mode              CFB128
Key               e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab e6 60 0f d8 85 2e f5 ab
IV                62 f0 15 15 b4 18 d5 55 f7 52 5e 42 51 0b 77 e8
Plaintext         48 34 45 36 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00
Ciphertext        36 3f 5e 89 9a f7 40 89 85 7a 95 cd 47 d7 2b 1a


The second fragment, after encryption, looks like the following.
Fragment 2 payload               62 f0 15 15 b4 18 d5 55 f7 52 5e 42 51 0b 77 e8
Fragment 2 CRC                   36 3f 5e 89

Appendix II Example of Multilinear Modular Hash (MMH) Algorithm
            Implementation (Informative)
This appendix gives an example implementation of the MMH MAC algorithm defined in Section 11.7.2. There may
be other implementations that have advantages over this example—in particular, operating environments. This
example is for informational purposes only and does not completely follow the DOCSIS MMH MAC algorithm
defined in Section 11.7.3.
A main program is included for exercising the example implementation. The output produced by the program is
included.

// Example code for MMH algorithm as defined in the DOCSIS 3.0 security specification

// Based on code in PacketCable security specification

// Original code by Mike Sabin
// DOCSIS modifications by Doc Evans, N7DR@arrisi.com
// This code is far from optimal; it is intended to be easy to follow, not fast to
execute

// Written for gcc 3.3.6

#include <iomanip>
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

#include <inttypes.h>
#include <stdio.h>

// Define this symbol to see intermediate values.
#define VERBOSE

// Define this symbol if you want to duplicate the PacketCable test vectors
#undef PACKETCABLE

// save ourselves some typing
using namespace std;

// Routine to reduce an int32_t value modulo F4, where F4 = 0x10001.
// Result is therefore in range [0, 0x10000].
const int32_t reduceModF4(const int32_t x)
{ int32_t rv = x;                             // holder for return value

// If x is negative, add a multiple of F4 to make it non-negative.
// This loop executes no more than twice.
  while (rv < 0)
    rv += 0x7fff7fff;

// Subtract high 16 bits of rv from low 16 bits.
  const int32_t xHi = rv >> 16;
  const int32_t xLo = rv & 0xffff;
  rv = xLo - xHi;

// If x is negative, add F4.
  if (rv < 0)
    rv += 0x10001;

// we are done
  return rv;
}




09/03/25                                         CableLabs®                                                   241
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                       Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


/*
Compute and return the MMH16 MAC of the message using the
indicated key and one-time pad.
The length of the key is to be at least msgLen bytes.
The length of the pad is at least two bytes.
*/
const uint16_t mmh16(const string& message,
                      const string& key,
                      const string& pad)
{
// check lengths
   if (key.length() < message.length())
     throw exception();

  if (pad.length() < 2)
    throw exception();

// ok to proceed
  int32_t sum = 0;         // 32-bit accumulator

  for (unsigned int i = 0; i < message.length(); i += 2)
  {
// Build a 16-bit factor from the next two message octets
    const int16_t x = (static_cast<uint16_t>(static_cast<unsigned char>(message[i]))
<< 8)
                    |
                       static_cast<uint16_t>(static_cast<unsigned char>(message[i +
1]));

// Build a 16-bit factor from the next two key octets
    const int16_t y = (static_cast<uint16_t>(static_cast<unsigned char>(key[i])) << 8)
|
                       static_cast<uint16_t>(static_cast<unsigned char>(key[i + 1]));

// Accumulate product of the factors into 32-bit sum
    sum += (static_cast<int32_t>(x) * static_cast<int32_t>(y));

#if defined(VERBOSE)
    cout << hex << " x 0x" << setw(2) << x
                << " y 0x" << setw(2) << y
                << " sum 0x" << setw(2) << sum << dec << endl;
#endif    // VERBOSE
  }

// Reduce sum modulo F4 and truncate to 16 bits
  uint16_t u = static_cast<uint16_t>(reduceModF4(sum));

#if defined(VERBOSE)
  cout << hex << " sum mod F4, truncated to 16 bits: 0x" << setw(2) << u << dec <<
endl;
#endif    // VERBOSE

// Build the pad variable from the two pad bytes
  const uint16_t v = (static_cast<uint16_t>(static_cast<unsigned char>(pad[0])) << 8)
|
                      static_cast<uint16_t>(static_cast<unsigned char>(pad[1]));

#if defined(VERBOSE)
  cout << hex << " pad variable: 0x" << setw(2) << v << dec << endl;
#endif    // VERBOSE

/* Accumulate pad variable, truncate to 16 bits */
  u = static_cast<uint16_t>(u + v);



242                                    CableLabs®                                     09/03/25
Security Specification                                          CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


#if defined(VERBOSE)
  cout << hex << " mmh16 value: 0x" << setw(2) << u << dec << endl;
#endif    // VERBOSE
  return u;
}

/*
Compute and return the MMH32 MAC of the message using the
indicated key and pad.
The length of the message is msgLen bytes; msgLen is to be even.
The length of the key is to be at least (msgLen + 2) bytes.
The length of the pad is four bytes. The pad is to be freshly
picked from a secure random source.
*/
const uint32_t mmh32(const string& message,
                      const string& key,
                      const string& pad)
{
// check lengths
   if (key.length() < (message.length() + 2))
     throw exception();

    if (pad.length() < 4)
      throw exception();

    const uint16_t x = mmh16(message, key, pad);
    const uint16_t y = mmh16(message, key.substr(2), pad.substr(2));
    const uint32_t sum = (static_cast<const uint32_t>(x) << 16) |
                          static_cast<const uint32_t>(y);

    return sum;
}

/*
Compute and return the MMH64 MAC of the message using the
indicated key and pad.
The length of the message is msgLen bytes; msgLen is to be even.
The length of the key is to be at least (msgLen + 6) bytes.
The length of the pad is eight bytes. The pad is to be freshly
picked from a secure random source.
*/
const uint64_t mmh64(const string& message,
                      const string& key,
                      const string& pad)
{
// check lengths
   if (key.length() < (message.length() + 6))
     throw exception();

    if (pad.length() < 8)
      throw exception();

    const uint16_t a = mmh16(message, key, pad);
    const uint16_t b = mmh16(message, key.substr(2), pad.substr(2));
    const uint16_t c = mmh16(message, key.substr(4), pad.substr(4));
    const uint16_t d = mmh16(message, key.substr(6), pad.substr(6));

    const uint64_t sum = (((((static_cast<const uint64_t>(a) << 16) |
                              static_cast<const uint64_t>(b)) << 16) |
                          static_cast<const uint64_t>(c)) << 16) |
                          static_cast<const uint64_t>(d);

    return sum;



09/03/25                                CableLabs®                                  243
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                          Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


}

/*
Routine to display a byte array
*/
template<class T>
void show(const string& rubric, const T& src, const unsigned int len)
{ const unsigned int BYTES_PER_LINE = 16;

    cout << rubric;

    for (unsigned int i = 0; i < len; i++)
    { if ((i % BYTES_PER_LINE) == 0)
        cout << endl;

        cout << setw(2) << hex << (unsigned int)(unsigned char)(src[i]) << " ";
    }

    cout << endl;
}

// helper function for displaying results
template<class T>
const string output_octets(const T& src)
{ string rv;
  const unsigned char* cp = (const unsigned char*)(&src);

    for (int n = sizeof(src) - 1; n >= 0; n--)
      rv += cp[n];

    return rv;
}

// example of use
int main(void)
{
// set formatting for cout
  cout.fill('0');

    string key_;
    string pad_;

// define some test vectors

#if !defined(PACKETCABLE)
// a trivial keystream
  for (unsigned int key_nr = 0; key_nr < 100; key_nr++)
    key_ += char(key_nr);

// a similarly trivial one-time pad (that is much longer than we need);
  for (unsigned int pad_nr = 0; pad_nr < 100; pad_nr++)
    pad_ += char(pad_nr + 1);

// an historically interesting message that is to be hashed
  const string message_("The Magic Words are Squeamish Ossifrage");
#endif     // !PACKETCABLE

#if defined(PACKETCABLE)
  const string message_("Now is the time.");

    unsigned char key[] = {
    0x35, 0x2c, 0xcf, 0x84, 0x95, 0xef, 0xd7, 0xdf, 0xb8,
    0xf5, 0x74, 0x05, 0x95, 0xeb, 0x98, 0xd6, 0xeb, 0x98,



244                                       CableLabs®                                     09/03/25
Security Specification                                          CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


    };

    unsigned char pad16[] = {
    0xae, 0x07,
    };

    unsigned char pad32[] = {
    0xbd, 0xe1, 0x89, 0x7b,
    };

    for (unsigned int key_nr = 0; key_nr < sizeof(key); key_nr++)
      key_ += key[key_nr];

  for (unsigned int pad_nr = 0; pad_nr < sizeof(pad16); pad_nr++)
    pad_ += pad16[pad_nr];
#endif    // PACKETCABLE

// MMH16
  cout << "Example of MMH16 computation" << endl;

    show("message", message_, message_.length());
    show("key", key_, message_.length());
    show("pad", pad_, 2);

    const uint16_t mac16 = mmh16(message_, key_, pad_);

    show("MMH16 MAC", output_octets(mac16), sizeof(mac16));
    cout << endl;

#if defined(PACKETCABLE)
  pad_.clear();
  for (unsigned int apad_nr = 0; apad_nr < sizeof(pad32); apad_nr++)
    pad_ += pad32[apad_nr];
#endif    // PACKETCABLE

// MMH32
  cout << "Example of MMH32 computation" << endl;

    show("message", message_, message_.length());
    show("key", key_, message_.length() + 2);
    show("pad", pad_, 4);

    const uint32_t mac32 = mmh32(message_, key_, pad_);

    show("MMH32 MAC", output_octets(mac32), sizeof(mac32));
    cout << endl;

#if !defined(PACKETCABLE)
// MMH64
  cout << "Example of MMH64 computation" << endl;

    show("message", message_, message_.length());
    show("key", key_, message_.length() + 6);
    show("pad", pad_, 8);

    const uint64_t mac64 = mmh64(message_, key_, pad_);

  show("MMH64 MAC", output_octets(mac64), sizeof(mac64));
  cout << endl;
#endif    // !PACKETCABLE

    return 0;
}

Appendix III Certificate Authority and Provisioning Guidelines
             (Informative)
III.1        DOCSIS PKI, Certificate Format, and Extensions
This appendix provides detailed information about the profiles (configuration of certificates) supported in DOCSIS
4.0 technology that are meant to foster interoperability and provide support for advanced cryptographic algorithms
and key sizes.
The DOCSIS 4.0 specification leverages the same CableLabs PKI hierarchy that is used to authenticate DOCSIS 3.1
devices.
The description of the PKI, together with its configuration, is described in Section 5 of the CableLabs Trust
Infrastructure document [C-PKI-TI].

III.1.1 Trust Infrastructure Document and DOCSIS 4.0 Technology

The Trust Infrastructure Document [C-PKI-TI] provides a normative description of all the profiles that are allowed
to be issued from the DOCSIS PKI. As such, the document provides a superset of the different options detailed in
the relevant specifications and serves as a useful tool to simplify compliance checking with the certificate policy.
To provide backward compatibility, interoperability, and ease of use, this appendix details a set of three certificate
profiles for use with DOCSIS 4.0 devices where the available options have been selected to deliver specific features.
For example, a common certificate profile (CCP) is defined to provide support for a single certificate solution across
DOCSIS 3.1 and 4.0 devices. Additionally, two separate profiles for a CMTS (i.e., the full certificate profile and the
no-revocation information profile) allow enabling or disabling revocation checking for BPI+ V2.
As needed, DOCSIS 4.0 certificates use values for object identifiers (or OIDs) that are managed under the IANA-
assigned CableLabs OID arc (1.3.6.1.4.4491).
The CableLabs OID arc organization and management are described in Section 5.3 of [C-PKI-TI].

III.2        DOCSIS 4.0 CM Certificate Profiles
CM device certificates are issued by device certification authorities to DOCSIS 4.0 certified cable modems. These
certificates are used to authenticate CMs to the network via BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2.
The profiles adopted for DOCSIS 4.0 CMs are defined in this section.

III.2.1 DOCSIS 4.0 CM Common Certificate Profile (CCP)

The common certificate profile for CMs is meant to provide a single certificate across all supported authentication
modes (i.e., BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2). Since BPI+ V1 is shared across DOCSIS 3.1 devices and DOCSIS 4.0 devices,
the CM Common Certificate Profile, described in Table 64, leverages fields and cryptographic options that are
backward compatible with the DOCSIS 3.1 environment.
Please refer to Section 7.2 of this document for more information about key management message formats and their
size limitations (both for device and CA certificates).
                         Table 64 - CableLabs DOCSIS 4.0 CM Common Certificate Profile

                                             o=CableLabs
                                             ou=Device CA<ID#>
                                             cn=CableLabs Device Certification Authority

Other non-critical extensions might be used in device certificates as needed.

III.3      DOCSIS 4.0 CMTS Certificate Profiles
CMTS device certificates are issued by device certification authorities for DOCSIS 4.0 cable modem termination
systems (or CMTS). These certificates are used to authenticate the CMTS during BPI+ V2 authentications and are
not used during BPI+ V1 authentications.
The CMTS Full Certificate Profile is used when CMTS revocation checking on CMs is required; in this case, the
presence of the authorityInfoAccess extension triggers the CM revocation checking requirements.
The CMTS NRI Certificate Profile is used when CMTS revocation checking on CMs is disabled. In this case, the
lack of the authorityInfoAccess extensions does not trigger the CM revocation checking requirements.
Operators and manufacturers may decide to install both versions of the profiles (dual certificates) to allow for easily
enabling or disabling revocation checking controls for BPI+ V2.
Because devices that provide DOCSIS 4.0 CMTS functionalities, such as RPHY CCAP Cores or FMA MAC-NEs,
have to include the svcCMTS value in their certificate's extended key usage (EKU) extension to be recognized by
DOCSIS 4.0 CMs as described in Section 7.1.1.2.2, CMTS certificates always include the svcCMTS value in the
EKU.

III.3.1 DOCSIS 4.0 CMTS Full Certificate Profile

The CMTS Full Certificate Profile provides all the required extensions to support CMTS certificate revocation
checking. See Section 7.1.1.2 for more information about how the relevant extensions are processed in DOCSIS 4.0
systems.
The DOCSIS 4.0 CMTS Full Certificate Profile is provided in Table 65.

III.3.2 DOCSIS 4.0 CMTS No-Revocation Information (NRI) Certificate Profile

The CMTS NRI Certificate Profile is used when support for CMTS certificate revocation checking on CMs is not
desired. Specifically, while the CMTS can be configured to enable or disable checking the revocation status of CMs,
the CM does not have such controls and the use of the CMTS full or NRI profiles drives the device behavior. See
Section 7.1.1.2 for more information about how the relevant extensions are processed in DOCSIS 4.0 systems.

III.4         Code Verification Certificate
Code verification certificates (CVCs) are used to authenticate software images. The profile for code verification
certificates is provided in [C-PKI-TI] Section 12.1. When compared to previous environments, DOCSIS 4.0 CVC
certificates allow for specifying an extra value in the organizational unit field of the certificate (optional) that can be
used for additional validation procedures such as restricting its use to a specific environment or enabling additional
checks during certificate validation.
DOCSIS 4.0 CVC certificates leverage the full set of the SHA-2 hashing algorithms family (i.e., SHA-256,
SHA-384, and SHA-512) and can use RSA keys of sizes up to 4096 bits.

III.5         CableLabs DOCSIS Service Provider Certificate
Service provider certificates are used to authenticate the operator's DOCSIS infrastructure (e.g., AAA servers, TLS
servers, etc.). For example, an AAA DOCSIS service provider certificate can be used to enable RadSec or TLS-
based authentication between devices and infrastructure or to provide TLS for SSH authentication servers.
Service provider certificates allow flexible configurations by offering many optional features (e.g., extensions,
subject name fields, etc.) that operators can choose from (refer to sections 12.2 and 12.3 of [C-PKI-TI] for more
information).

III.6         Certificate Installation
The following certificates are installed in the CMTS by either the supplier during manufacturing or cable operator
during installation.
      •   CableLabs root CA certificate (used for validating CM certificates and firmware upgrades)
      •   The issuing CableLabs device CA certificate (used to chain to the CMTS to the CableLabs root CA
          certificate)
      •   CMTS device certificate together with its corresponding private key
The following certificates are installed in the CM during manufacturing.
      •   CableLabs root CA certificate (used for validating code download images)
      •   The issuing CableLabs device CA certificate (used to chain the CM to the CableLabs root CA certificate)
      •   CM device certificate and its corresponding private key



256                                                   CableLabs®                                                 09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                 CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


III.7         CM Code File Signing Policy and Format
CM vendors and cable operators can control the secure software download process based on their policies by
updating the manufacturer/co-signer CVC or by changing the signingTime in the manufacturer/co-signer code
verification signature (CVS). At this time, the DOCSIS 4.0 specifications do not specify the policy related to the CM
code file signing process. However, an example of the policy is specified in this section.

III.7.1 Manufacturer CM Code File Signing Policy

In order to sign code files, the manufacturer obtains a valid CVC from the DOCSIS root CA.
When signing a code file, a manufacturer may choose not to update the FWAH (i.e., [PKCS#7]) signingTime
value in the manufacturer signing information. The FWAH signingTime value should be equal to or greater than the
CVC's validity start time. The FWAH signingTime value is not less than the CVC's validity start time.
The CM vendor and its manufacturer code signing agent (Mfg CSA), which securely stores the RSA private key
corresponding to the RSA public key in the manufacturer CVC and generates the CVS for the CM code file, might
employ the following policy for the CM code file signing process.
The Mfg CSA continues to put exactly the same date and time value (T1) in the signingTime field in the Mfg CVS
of the CM code file as long as the vendor does not have any CM code file to revoke.
Once the vendor realizes there are certain issues in one or more CM code file(s) and wants to revoke them, the
vendor chooses the current date and time value (T2) and starts using T2 as the signingTime value in the Mfg CVS
for all the newly created CM code file from that point. In addition, any CM code files signed with T1 that are still
good are re-signed using T2.
Under this policy, because the multiple CM code files make a group of the CM code files with the exact same
signingTime value in the Msg CVS, the operator can download any CM code file in the group in any order. That is,
among the CM code files in the same group, the CM's software can be downgraded if necessary.

III.7.2 Operator CM Code File Signing Policy

Operators should verify that the code image as received from a vendor is exactly as built by the trusted manufacturer.
The operator may choose to co-sign code images destined for use on its network.
All code images downloaded to a CM across the network are signed in accordance with this specification.

III.7.3 CM Code File Format

A single file is used to encapsulate the code for the cable modem. The CM code file is composed of a firmware
authentication header (FWAH) and a SignedContent field.
The signed data message includes:
    •    the manufacturer code verification signature (CVS);
    •    the manufacturer code verification certificate (CVC);
    •    the issuing CVC CA certificate;
    •    the code image (compatible with the destination CM) as signed content;
    •    optionally, when the MSO co-signs the code file:
         a)       the co-signer CVS;
         b)       the co-signer CVC; and
         c)       the issuing CVC CA certificate; this certificate does not need to be separately added when it is
                  identical to the issuing CVC CA certificate of the manufacturer CVC;
    •    optional device CA certificate; and
    •    optional root CA certificate.


09/03/25                                             CableLabs®                                                      257
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                                    Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


The code file complies with [PKCS#7] and is DER encoded. The code file matches the structure shown below. A
code file example is shown in Annex D.
                                                     Table 67 - CM Code File

       Code File Structure                                                     Description
  [PKCS#7] Digital Signature{
  ContentInfo
         contentType                SignedData
         signedData()               EXPLICIT signed-data content value; includes CVS and [X.509] CVC
   }
  SignedContent{
   DownloadParameters {             Mandatory TLV format (Type 28) defined in the Section 7.2.2.28 (length is zero if there are no
                                    sub-TLVs).
         DeviceCACert()             Optional TLV for one DER-encoded 2nd GEN DOCSIS PKI certificate formatted according to
                                    the Device-CA-Certificate TLV format (Type 53) defined in Section 7.2.2.29.
         RootCACert()               Optional TLV for one DER-encoded 2nd GEN DOCSIS PKI certificate formatted according to
                                    the Root-CA-Certificate TLV format (Type 54) defined in Section 7.2.2.30.
   }
   CodeImage()                      Upgrade code image
   }


III.7.3.1 DOCSIS PKCS#7 Signed Data

The signedData field of the DOCSIS [PKCS#7] digital signature matches the DER encoded structure defined in
Appendix III.

III.7.3.1.1       Code Signing Keys

The digital signature uses the RSA encryption algorithm [RSA3] with SHA-256 [FIPS 180-4]. The RSA key
modulus is at least 2048 bits in length.
                                          Table 68 - DOCSIS PKCS#7 Signed Data

               PKCS#7 Field                                                       Description
  signedData {
       Version                              version = 1
       digestAlgorithmIdentifiers           SHA-256
       contentInfo
         contentType                        data (SignedContent is concatenated at the end of the [PKCS#7] structure)
       certificates {                       DOCSIS code verification certification (CVC)
         mfgCVC()                           Required for all code files
        mfgCVCCA()                          Required for all code files using the 2nd GEN DOCSIS PKI
         cosignerCVC()                      Optional; required for cable operator co-signatures
         cosignerCVCCA()                    Optional; required for cable operator co-signatures using the 2nd GEN DOCSIS PKI
                                            when cosignerCVCCA certificate is not identical to the mfgCVCCA certificate.
       } end certificates
       SignerInfo{
         MfgSignerInfo {                    Required for all code files
           Version                          version = 1
           issuerAndSerialNumber            from the signer's certificate
             issuerName                     distinguished name of the certificate issuer

Security Specification                                                                     CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


              PKCS#7 Field                                                   Description
         certificateSerialNumber        from CVC; integer, size (1..20) octets
       digestAlgorithm                  SHA-256
       authenticatedAttributes
         contentType                    data; contentType of signedContent
         signingTime                    UTCTime (GMT), YYMMDDhhmmssZ
         messageDigest                  digest of the content as defined in [PKCS#7]
       digestEncryptionAlgorithm        rsaEncryption
       encryptedDigest
       } end mfg signer info
      MsoSignerInfo {                   OPTIONAL; required for cable operator co-signatures
       Version                          version =1
       issuerAndSerialNumber            from the signer's certificate
         issuerName                     distinguished name of the certificate issuer
         certificateSerialNumber        from CVC; integer, size (1..20) octets
       digestAlgorithm                  SHA-256
       authenticatedAttributes
         contentType                    data; contentType of signedContent
         signingTime                    UTCTime (GMT), YYMMDDhhmmssZ
         messageDigest                  digest of the content as defined in [PKCS#7]
       digestEncryptionAlgorithm        rsaEncryption
       encryptedDigest
       } end mso signer info
     } end signer info
   } end signed data


III.7.3.1.2    Code Verification Certificate Format

The format used for the CVC is defined in Appendix III.4.

III.7.3.1.3    Code Verification Certificate Revocation

In addition to standard certificate revocation, there is a method for revoking CVCs based on the validity start date of the
certificate. This method requires that an updated CVC be delivered to the cable modem with an updated validity start
time. Once the CVC is successfully validated, the validity start time will update the CM's current value of
cvcAccessStart (see Section 14.3.2.2).
To expedite the delivery of an updated CVC without requiring the cable modem to process a code upgrade, the CVC
may be delivered in either the CM's configuration file or an SNMP MIB. The format of a DOCSIS CVC is the same
whether it is in a code file, configuration file, or SNMP MIB.

III.7.3.2 Signed Content

The SignedContent field of the code file contains the CodeImage and the DownloadParameters fields, which may
contain up to two items: a root CA certificate and a device CA certificate.
The final code image is in a binary format compatible with the destination CM. In support of the [PKCS#7]
signature requirements, the code content is encoded as an OCTET STRING.
Each manufacturer should build their code with additional mechanisms to verify that an upgrade code image is
compatible with the destination CM.




09/03/25                                                CableLabs®                                                    259
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                         Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


Appendix IV Considerations on DOCSIS 4.0 Deployments (Informative)
IV.1          From DOCSIS 3.1 to DOCSIS 4.0 Security: What Does NOT Change
DOCSIS 4.0 technology supports two versions of the BPI+ authentication protocol: BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2. Whereas
the latest version of the protocol (BPI+ V2) is only available in DOCSIS 4.0 mode, the first version of the protocol,
i.e., BPI+ V1, was introduced in the DOCSIS 1.1 specifications and continues in the DOCSIS 4.0 specifications. A
first advantage for retaining support for BPI+ V1 in the DOCSIS 4.0 specification is the ability to deploy new
DOCSIS 4.0 devices without the need for updating existing procedures, thus reducing the extra overhead of
deploying new technologies that might come with new requirements. A second advantage is that with BPI+ V1, the
CMTS does not need a device certificate; hence, a DOCSIS 4.0 deployment with BPI+ V1 does not introduce new
requirements (for security) when compared to previous DOCSIS versions. However, as discussed previously, with
the introduction of distributed nodes outside the operator's trusted domain, the attack surface has increased. When
enhanced authentications are needed, BPI+ V2 can be enabled to address the new security risks.
From the secure software download (SSD) standpoint, the DOCSIS 4.0 specification both maintains support for
current procedures and provides the possibility for migrating to a more efficient procedure when needed. The first
procedure is the same as that leveraged in the DOCSIS 3.1 specification where the use of a manufacturer's CVC
and/or an operator's co-signer CVC certificate(s), in the config file (or via SNMP set), triggers SSD procedures. A
second mechanism was added that uses the firmware authentication header (FWAH).
Another important similarity between the DOCSIS 3.1 and DOCSIS 4.0 specifications is the use of the same PKI.
The DOCSIS 4.0 PKI uses the same root of trust and the same procedures as the DOCSIS 3.1 PKI to validate device
certificates.
When looking at backward compatibility, a DOCSIS 4.0 CM includes two certificates. The first is a DOCSIS 4.0
CM certificate that uses a Common Cable Modem certificate profile to connect to either a DOCSIS 4.0 or a DOCSIS
3.1 CMTS. DOCSIS 4.0 certificates are different from DOCSIS 3.1 certificates, and a DOCSIS 3.1 CMTS might
need a software upgrade to support DOCSIS 4.0 CMs. A DOCSIS 4.0 CM uses a DOCSIS 3.0 CM certificate to
connect to DOCSIS 3.0 and earlier CMTS. The reason for this is that DOCSIS 3.0 and DOCSIS 4.0 CM device
certificates do not share the same root of trust and, therefore, separate certificates are still needed.
The DOCSIS 4.0 specification also supports the CM certificate revocation methods that were used in both the
DOCSIS 3.1 and DOCSIS 3.0 specifications. When BPI+ V2 is enabled, the CMTS can use either CRLs or OCSPs
to check the status of a CM certificate and take proper action about it (or just report it). Support for revocation status
checking for the network (CMTS) certificate is discussed in the next section.
DOCSIS 4.0 technology support for EAE is backward compatible with previous versions of DOCSIS technology
where the CMTS support for EAE is advertised in the downstream MDD message. In the DOCSIS 4.0 MDD
message, the usual TLV Type 6 that advertises EAE support for BPI+ V1 is joined by the new Type 23 TLV that
specifies additional options for BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2, as described in the next section.
In summary, the security features and operations that remain common to both DOCSIS 3.1 and DOCSIS 4.0
technology are as follows.
      •   DOCSIS 4.0 technology can use the same BPI+ V1 authentication protocol as DOCSIS 3.1 technology.
      •   DOCSIS 4.0 technology uses the same PKI as DOCSIS 3.1 technology, the 2nd GEN DOCSIS PKI.
      •   DOCSIS 4.0 technology can use the same SSD procedures as DOCSIS 3.1 technology.
      •   DOCSIS 4.0 CMs require, exactly as DOCSIS 3.1 devices, an additional certificate from the 1st GEN
          DOCSIS PKI to authenticate in DOCSIS 3.0 networks (if the device supports D3.0 environments).
      •   DOCSIS 4.0 supports the same CM certificate revocation options as in DOCSIS 3.1 and DOCSIS 3.0
          technology.




260                                                  CableLabs®                                                 09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                  CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


IV.2         From DOCSIS 3.1 to DOCSIS 4.0 Security: What Changes
BPI+ V1 has been available since the DOCSIS 1.1 specification. The DOCSIS 4.0 specification is the first to
support BPI+ V2, which includes new and enhanced security controls.
On the certificate side, there are three important changes that need to be discussed.
First, DOCSIS 4.0 technology uses the existing DOCSIS 3.1 PKI, but the contents of CM certificates for the two
environments are different. Whereas a DOCSIS 4.0 CM certificate uses the same algorithm (RSA) and key sizes
(2048 bit) as a DOCSIS 3.1 CM certificate, DOCSIS 4.0 CM certificates are larger than their DOCSIS 3.1
counterparts because of the introduction of several extensions that deliver new security controls. The first change of
note is the use of the authority information access (AIA) extension to carry the location of the authoritative OCSP
server that the CMTS can use to check for the revocation status of CM certificates. In both DOCSIS 3.1 and
DOCSIS 3.0 security, the absence of such data makes revocation checking very hard in practice. Another important
change in the DOCSIS 4.0 CM certificate profile is related to the introduction of a new concept: roles or functions.
DOCSIS 4.0 certificates use a set of object identifiers inside the extended key usage (EKU) extension that allow the
verifier to check, based on the presence of specific values in the extension, if the CM is authorized to provide
specific services. DOCSIS 4.0 security defines OIDs to identify CMTS functionality (svcCMTS) and CM
functionality (svcCM). Table 69 provides the list of the EKU values supported in DOCSIS 4.0 certificates. For
example, when a CM is validating the CMTS certificate, it will look for the svcCM value in the EKU extension of
the certificate and, if not found, reject the connection to that CMTS.
                  Table 69 - Object Identifiers for EKU-Enabled Functionalities in DOCSIS 4.0

    Short Name                  Name                                 Value                        Description
    svcCMTS           id-cl-pki-eku-CMTS             1.3.6.1.4.1.4491.2021.2.1.1               CMTS functionalities
    svcCM             id-cl-pki-eku-CM               1.3.6.1.4.1.4491.2021.2.1.2               CM functionalities



Second, before the operator enables BPI+ V2, the CMTS needs to be provisioned with a DOCSIS certificate. The
use of CMTS credentials enables the CMTS to authenticate its own messages; therefore, turning on BPI+ V2 both
lowers the risks of network compromise and enables trusted services from the network enabled by the possibility to
validate, and subsequently trust, the CMTS identity. The provisioning and management of CMTS certificates (i.e.,
renewal and installation) is new in the DOCSIS 4.0 specification and is expected to require dedicated support and/or
automation. CMTS certificates have a validity of up to five years.
Third, a DOCSIS 4.0 CM uses a single certificate to authenticate in both DOCSIS 4.0 and DOCSIS 3.1 modes. This
means that a DOCSIS 4.0 CM connecting to a DOCSIS 3.1 CMTS will use a certificate that, though compatible
with the DOCSIS 3.1 environment (i.e., same algorithm and key sizes), can be larger in size than a DOCSIS 3.1 CM
certificate because a DOCSIS 4.0 CM certificate contains additional extensions that are not present in a DOCSIS 3.1
CM certificate.
On the CVC side, there are also important updates. DOCSIS 4.0 security does not support CVCs from the 1st GEN
DOCSIS PKI for secure software download and firmware signing. Because the validation of 1st GEN DOCSIS PKI
CVCs does not require checking of expiration times against the current time, retaining support for 1st GEN DOCSIS
PKI CVCs would introduce the possibility to modify the code on DOCSIS 4.0 CMs with CVCs that use outdated
(and possibly weak) cryptographic parameters such as 1024-bit keys and the SHA-1 algorithm for signatures. Hence,
DOCSIS 4.0 CMs only support CVCs from the 2nd GEN DOCSIS PKI. Additionally, a new mechanism to initiate
the SSD process has been introduced that optimized validations of the firmware by directly supporting the use of the
firmware authentication header (FWAH) in CM configuration files or via SNMP set commands.
On the revocation side, there are also updates. When BPI+ V2 is enabled and revocation checking is in force, the
CM validates the CMTS certificate, known as mutual authentication. With BPI+ V2, the CMTS transmits the OCSP
response related to the status of its own certificate in the Auth Reply or Auth Reject message to the CM. The
CMTS's OCSP response can be cached by the CM and the CMTS for its entire validity time to minimize the load on
revocation infrastructures.
There are also updates to EAE. Though support for the CMTS MDD message TLV Type 6, i.e., the EAE
enabled/disable TLV, is maintained for backward compatibility, DOCSIS 4.0 devices use the new MDD message



09/03/25                                            CableLabs®                                                        261
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                       Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


TLV Type 23, i.e., BPI+ Supported Version and Configuration, to discover which BPI+ version and services are
enabled on the CMTS. This new TLV is a compound TLV that uses two bytes to indicate (a) the enabled version of
BPI (i.e., 1-byte integer value), and (b) the associated enabled features (i.e., bitmask where Bit 7, when set to one,
indicates EAE support).
On the BPKM protocol, DOCSIS 4.0 introduces larger message sizes with the availability of fragmentation to
extend the supported maximum size of BPKM payloads. This change is implemented by introducing two new
MMM messages, the version 5 BPKM-REQ and the BPKM-RSP, that support BPKM messages that can span more
than one frame. This solution enables both the use of additional cryptographic material in the Auth Request/Auth
Reply messages without the need to add new messages and states, and it also opens future paths for new
cryptographic algorithms such as Kyber (for key exchange) and Dilithium (for public/private keys), or even hybrid
approaches that combine RSA with new types of algorithms.
In summary, the security features and operations have been updated for DOCSIS 4.0 security are:
      •   support for both BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2;
      •   use of advanced authentication features when enabling BPI+ V2;
      •   DOCSIS 4.0 CMs use certificates that contain more information and are larger in size than D3.1
          certificates;
      •   updated SSD procedures that optimize early error detection;
      •   two new approaches for delivering SSH access to CMs without static secrets on devices;
      •   the ability to independently enable or disable, for each enabled BPI+ version, the use of EAE; and
      •   fragmentation support for BPI+ V2 messages.

IV.3          Updated Procedures for Certificate Revocation
Support for checking the status of revocation for DOCSIS devices has been available since the DOCSIS 3.0
specifications where both CRL and OCSP checking were introduced to lower security risks associated with
providing services to potentially compromised devices.
The DOCSIS 4.0 specification added two changes in the protocol that affected revocation checking procedures: the
introduction of the CMTS (or network) identity and updated certificate profiles.
The introduction of the network identity required the definition of a new security control that would allow a CM to
know when (or not) to demand OCSP responses from the CMTS. DOCSIS 4.0 CMs do not directly perform OCSP
queries. Because malicious actors could try to remove checking of revocation information to make it easier to use
compromised credentials, DOCSIS 4.0 technology does not leverage the usual control interface for configuring CM
revocation checking requirements (i.e., config file TLV or SNMP set), rather, it embeds the control in the CMTS
certificate. When CM revocation checking is desired, the CMTS certificate will contain the URL of the OCSP
responder, i.e., the authority info access extension with the OCSP access method. When the URL is embedded in the
CMTS certificate, the CM understands that revocation status checking is required and will reject messages that do
not carry the needed OCSP responses for the CMTS certificate. And when revocation checking is not to be required
of the CM, the CMTS certificate will not contain the OCSP URL. When the URL of the OCSP responder is not
embedded in the CMTS certificate, the CM understands that revocation checking is not required; therefore, OCSP
responses are not needed in the CMTS's messages. CMTS certificates that do not carry any OCSP revocation
information are referred to as NRI certificates, or no-revocation information certificates.
In other words, the protected value, i.e., the extension, inside the CMTS certificate is the secure equivalent of
configuring revocation checking by a CM because the presence of the extension in the certificate is protected by the
CA signature on the device certificate itself (i.e., even the CMTS cannot lie about the requirement).
In this view, a CMTS could support a dual-certificates configuration for its devices: one certificate for when CMTS
certificate revocation checking is enabled and one certificate for when CMTS certificate revocation checking is
disabled.




262                                                 CableLabs®                                                 09/03/25
Security Specification                                                               CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


Figure 23 provides an overview of the generic workflow for the BPI+ protocol basic authentication messages when
combined with the revocation information checking process.




                           Figure 23 - Integrated BPI+ and Revocation Checking Flow


As depicted in Figure 23, the retrieval of the revocation information can be triggered by new Authorization Requests
coming into the CMTS; however, it might be a good practice to keep a caching mechanism on the CMTS to allow
for faster authentications (and re-authentications) and reduce the number of external requests the CMTS issues (and
relies upon) to complete the authentication. This is particularly true in the context of OCSP processing when a
CMTS is rebooting and, in general, for CRL-based revocation checking where usually the validity period of a CRL
is quite long (days or weeks). Because DOCSIS 4.0 technology explicitly allows for caching the revocation
information for their entire validity period, it is important to leverage this option on the CMTS to deliver fast and
efficient revocation status checking.
Another important consideration is related to the CMTS's ability to directly connect to resources on the Internet and
not available on the operator's private network. Because of security concerns, CMTSs are not allowed to access any
resources on the Internet. When this is the case, the CMTS would not be able to query CRL repositories or OCSP
responders unless some "intermediary" is used. For example, operators can route all requests for revocation
information via an HTTP proxy, thus allowing for easier monitoring (and restrictions) for which resources the proxy
can access (e.g., only official OCSP repositories).
However, when HTTP proxy services are not enough and operators would like to take control over the revocation
information for the devices deployed in their own network, operators can consider deploying local OCSP servers
that can provide local OCSP responses. For this option to be enabled, the operator needs an OCSP responder
certificate from the DOCSIS infrastructure (for each DOCSIS 4.0 issuing CA) that can be used to set up the local
service. Efficient open source implementations for inline OCSP responders are already available; e.g., OpenSSL
[OSSL] or OpenCA OCSPD [OCAOD].

IV.3.1 Enabling Mutual Authentication Without Revocation Checking

A simple and common deployment model is the one where there is no support for revocation status checking. This is
the default deployment scenario in today's DOCSIS network where revocation checking is disabled or practically
very difficult to implement because of the lack of OCSP URLs inside DOCSIS 3.0 and DOCSIS 3.1 certificates.




09/03/25                                           CableLabs®                                                    263
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                       Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


In this authentication mode, when either BPI+ V1 or V2 are instantiated, the CMTS does not download any CRL or
any OCSP responses to validate the revocation status of the device certificate. This means that authentications only
rely on the information presented by the device (or the network) to decide whether to allow the connection with the
device (or the network). This means that the extra steps {1.a, …, 1.f} from Figure 23 are not needed and will not be
executed in both BPI+ V1 and BPI+ V2.
To achieve this configuration, the CMTS is configured to disable both CRL and OCSP responses. Changing the
CMTS configuration is sufficient when only BPI+ V1 is enabled. However, to correctly handle the BPI+ V2 case,
the CMTS is also provisioned with a CMTS NRI certificate—the absence of the OCSP URL inside the certificate is
used as the security control to communicate to the CM that no revocation checking is needed on this certificate when
executing BPI+ V2 only. BPI+ V1 does not use any network identity and, therefore, there is no CMTS certificate to
validate.

IV.3.2 Enabling Mutual Authentication with CM's Certificate (Client-Side) Revocation Checking

As in both DOCSIS 3.0 and DOCSIS 3.1 technology, DOCSIS 4.0 technology supports checking the revocation
status of connected devices when using either BPI+ V1 or BPI+ V2. When using BPI+ V2, an NRI certificate is
installed on the CMTS. In this configuration, OCSP responses are not sent inline from the CMTS to the CM during
BPI+ authentications because NRI certificates do not carry the URL of an OCSP responder, thus limiting the
dependencies on the availability of revocation information to the CMTS only.
Because the CMTS is the ultimate controller that can allow or reject a CM during authentication, CMTS suppliers
can implement different authorization policies to better accommodate operational needs. For example, a CMTS
could provide the possibility to have strict or permissive policies for allowing devices on the network only after
passing revocation checking (strict policy) or allowing them even when revoked and, in that case, report it for
monitoring or investigative purposes (permissive policy). Though a strict revocation is the most secure option, e.g.,
not allowing compromised devices to access any service, permissive policies could be implemented to monitor for
potentially compromised or otherwise misbehaving devices. This approach allows for decoupling the decision to
provision services to devices from the certificate revocation status (i.e., the revoked status becomes a factor in the
decision, not the decision itself).
In this case, though both OCSP and CRL mechanisms can be used for checking the status of devices, it might be
more efficient to enable CRL-based validation because a single CRL carries all relevant revocation information for
all the certificates issued from the CA, whereas OCSP responses are related to a single certificate entry. When using
OCSP, individual request/response roundtrips are used for each CM for which the CMTS needs to validate the
revocation status, whereas a single CRL can be used to look up the revocation status of all certificates issued from a
CA (with the downside of being quite large if many certificates have been revoked).

IV.3.3 Enabling Mutual Authentication with CMTS's Certificate (Server-Side) Revocation Checking

This deployment model enables the revocation status checking for the CMTS certificate to authenticate the network.
This use case, because it involves the CMTS certificate, is relevant only for BPI+ V2 authentications.
To achieve this configuration, revocation checking is disabled on the CMTS, both CRL-based and OCSP-based
mechanisms, for the client side. By disabling revocation checking on the CMTS, the CMTS will not execute steps
{1.a, …, 1.f} from Figure 23 to validate the CM's certificate.
However, the CMTS still needs to procure the OCSP response for its own certificate and send it to the CM in the
Authorization Reply message when starting BPI+ V2. Therefore, the CMTS still needs to execute steps {1.d, 1.e,
1.f} to be able to retrieve the OCSP response from the server (if not cached).
Differently from the two previous use cases, the CMTS certificate carries the URL of the OCSP responder to
communicate to the CM that OCSP responses validation is required for this certificate. CMTS certificates that
contain revocation information are referred to as "full" or "full CMTS" certificates.
When validation checking is to be performed by the CM, the CMTS provides the OCSP response inline during BPI+
authentications, and that means that if a response cannot be fetched (or the cached version is expired), the CM will
reject the CMTS certificate.




264                                                 CableLabs®                                               09/03/25
Security Specification                                                                CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903


IV.3.4 Enabling Mutual Authentication with Mutual Revocation Checking

The last use case to explore is the scenario where both the client side (CM) and the server side (CMTS) are required
to check the validation status of the other party. This setting, as in the previous one, is only applicable when BPI+
V2 is used.
To achieve this setup, the CMTS is configured to enable the revocation checking of CM certificates either via CRLs
or OCSP. Moreover, the CMTS is provisioned with a full CMTS certificate to enable revocation checking by the
CM.
In this configuration, the authentication message flow will use steps {1.a, 1.b, and 1.c} to validate the CM's
certificate when the CRL mechanism is used or steps {1.d, 1.e, 1.f} to validate the CM's certificate when the OCSP
mechanism is enabled. Additionally, steps {1.d, 1.e, 1.f} are repeated to gather the revocation information of the
CMTS certificate that needs to be sent inline when starting BPI+ V2.

IV.4         Deployment Examples
As described above, DOCSIS 4.0 technology offers a series of new security features that can be independently
enabled to lower network security risks. This section provides an overview on how to support DOCSIS 4.0 CMs in
existing networks, e.g., DOCSIS 3.1 or earlier, and then transition to enabling the new security features when
deploying new DOCSIS 4.0-enabled networks. The envisioned architecture is depicted in Figure 24 where it is
assumed that the CMTS (i.e., or where the functionality is provided such as the CCAP Core or MAC-NE elements
in distributed architectures) does not have direct access to external services. In this architecture, the CMTS can route
all the HTTP requests for CRLs and OCSP processing via the HTTP proxy where strict access rules can be enforced.
Additionally, to improve network reliability, operators can deploy local OCSP proxies and/or responders that can
directly sign (or cache) valid responses locally. This solution can be used not only to improve network reliability,
but also to provide support for locally managed revocations that are specific for the operator's ecosystem such as
tracking (permissive policy) or reject (strict policy) cloned or otherwise potentially compromised devices.




           Figure 24 - Example Deployment with Revocation Checking Support and Local Overrides




09/03/25                                            CableLabs®                                                     265
CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903                                        Data-Over-Cable Service Interface Specifications


IV.4.1 Preparing Your Networks for DOCSIS 4.0 CMs and CMTS

To prepare the network for operating with DOCSIS 4.0 devices, there are important details that operators need to be
aware of for successful deployments planning.
First, DOCSIS 4.0 CM certificates are bigger in size than DOCSIS 3.1 CM certificates because of included OCSP
information. Because of this larger certificate, BPI+ V1 messages might exceed the 1490-byte size limit of DOCSIS
3.1 and earlier BPKM messages. Hence, a DOCSIS 3.1 CMTS likely needs a software update to enable processing
BPKM messages that are larger than 1490 bytes and the ability to parse this new certificate format. This issue does
not affect DOCSIS 3.0 and earlier CMTSs because a DOCSIS 4.0 CM is required to also have a separate certificate
issued from the 1st GEN DOCSIS PKI.
When operators are ready to deploy DOCSIS 4.0 CMTS, BPI+ V2 security can be used.

IV.4.2 Increasing Speeds with BPI+ V1

DOCSIS 4.0 networks can be configured to use BPI+ V1 with no need to change from the currently deployed
security model.
This feature allows the network to transition to BPI+ V2 when the operator is ready.
In other words, only when and if new security features are needed (i.e., PFS or MA), operators can choose to enable
the use of BPI+ V2 according to their own deployment plans and schedules.

IV.4.3 Enabling Advanced Security Features with BPI+ V2

The need for trusted networks, combined with the need to mitigate new attack vectors related to new distributed
deployment models, are two of the reasons for enabling BPI+ V2.
When the BPI+ V2 authentication protocol is enabled, a new set of controls is available to the operator via the
configuration of the persistent security attributes (PSAs). PSAs are stored in the secure memory of the CM and
allow operators to further restrict accepted network/CMTS identities and protocol versions during authentications
(i.e., via the Allowed-BPI-Versions TLV) and are used to implement the downgrade protection mechanisms
for BPI+. Additionally, PSA attributes are used to restrict what is considered valid with respect to network identities
by requiring, in the CMTS certificate, the presence of specific values. For example, it is possible to configure a CM
to only accept "Operator A" as the Organization field (O) in the certificate's subject name by using the CMTS-
Designations TLVs in the Auth Reply message from the CMTS.
Enabling BPI+ V2, however, requires the provisioning and management of the CMTS certificate, as discussed
earlier. This means that a new set of operation procedures will be needed, from installing the CMTS certificate (if
not already installed by the supplier) to regularly renewing it before expiration (i.e., once every five years). Though
not part of the DOCSIS 4.0 specifications, it is expected that automated certificate renewal protocols and tools will
be developed and integrated with the increased enablement of BPI+ V2 across networks.

IV.4.4 Enabling DOCSIS 4.0 Revocation Checking

DOCSIS 4.0 technology allows for flexible configurations when it comes to revocation status checking. In fact, it is
important to notice how enabling or disabling revocation status checking can be done independently from enabling
or disabling the use of BPI+ V2. This capability is key for empowering operators to choose the deployment path that
is relevant for their networks today and their migration path(s) tomorrow. In other words, because of the available
DOCSIS 4.0 security options, revocation checking is not considered a limiting factor for BPI+ V2 deployment.
Because revocation checking has not yet been enabled by the community, enabling support for it needs careful
planning and might need additional infrastructure services such as HTTP proxies or local OCSP responders, as
depicted in Figure 23, especially when requiring CMs to check revocation status of CMTS certificates.

Security Specification                                                                   CM-SP-SECv4.0-I08-250903
